                                                Systematic Enumeration of Fundamental
                                                      Quantities Involving Runs
                                                           in Binary Strings

                                                                Félix Balado and Guénolé C.M. Silvestre




arXiv:2602.10005v2 [math.CO] 11 Feb 2026
                                                                       School of Computer Science
                                                                        University College Dublin
                                                                             Belfield Campus
                                                                            Dublin 4, Ireland
                                                                              felix@ucd.ie
                                                                       guenole.silvestre@ucd.ie


                                                                                     Abstract

                                                    We give recurrences, generating functions and explicit exact expressions for the
                                                enumeration of fundamental quantities involving runs in binary strings. We first focus
                                                on enumerations concerning runs of ones, and we then analyse the same enumerations
                                                when runs of ones and runs of zeros are jointly considered. We give the connections
                                                between these two types of run enumeration, and with the problem of compositions. We
                                                also analyse the same enumerations with a Hamming weight constraint. We discuss
                                                which of the many number sequences that emerge from these problems are already
                                                known and listed in the OEIS. Additionally, we extend our main enumerative results
                                                to the probabilistic scenario in which binary strings are outcomes of independent and
                                                identically distributed Bernoulli variables.



                                           1    Introduction
                                           Runs in binary strings are essentially uninterrupted sequences of the same bit. In this paper
                                           we give recurrences, generating functions and explicit expressions for the enumeration of
                                           fundamental quantities involving runs in binary strings. A lot of prior work on this topic
                                           is probabilistic in nature, in which case runs of ones are typically called success runs [5]
                                           —whereas runs of zeros are called failure runs. The very first historical problem on runs,
                                           which was studied by de Moivre [17], was in fact a success runs problem. Whereas some
                                           authors focus on problems concerning success runs [5, 51, 23], others jointly consider success
                                           and failure runs [66, 18, 54, 7, 5].

                                                                                          1
We can see enumerative results for runs in binary strings as special cases of the above corpus
of research. However, in spite of the long history of the topic, a uniform and consistent
approach to enumerating runs-related quantities is currently lacking. Some authors have
provided generating functions for specific settings [26, 2, 57, 28, 60, 36], while others have
derived explicit (closed-form) expressions for some others [57, 37, 28, 41, 48] —either through
direct combinatorial analysis or through generating functions. Other authors have given
recurrence relations for different problems involving runs [3, 2, 7, 54, 28, 49, 48]. But many
relevant enumeration aspects have apparently not been considered yet.
Our goal here is to try to fill this gap by systematically studying the most fundamental
enumerations of quantities involving runs in binary strings within a common framework.
Our strategy always involves: 1) finding one or more recurrences for each of the problems at
hand; and 2) if possible, solving the recurrences to obtain exact explicit expressions by means
of generating functions. This is hardly a novel approach, but one that has been frequently
sidelined in favour of standard combinatorial analysis in the study of runs —which makes
a number of these problems harder. The use of generating functions to study runs harks
back to the earliest problem of this kind studied by de Moivre [17]. As far as we are aware,
Laplace [30, p. 421] was the first author who used recurrences —or finite difference equations,
as they were commonly known back then— to derive probability generating functions for
problems involving runs. Later authors, prominently Feller [18, Ch. XIII], also used this
approach to deal with runs, although sometimes with a definition of “run” different than the
one adopted here —see Remark 2 below.
In this work, we recover many established results —often, though not always, via more
streamlined derivations— while more frequently we offer novel contributions in the form
of recurrences, generating functions, and explicit expressions. Although we are aware of
the limitations of such explicit expressions, we do not pursue here asymptotic approxima-
tions. As we proceed, we highlight which of the enumerations that we address correspond to
known number sequences —meaning that they have been documented in Sloane’s On-Line
Encyclopedia of Integer Sequences (OEIS) [62].
Importantly, we also give the most basic connections between the enumerations of runs
of ones, runs of ones and/or zeros, and compositions. Some of these connections have been
partly identified by previous authors [6, 54, 28], but, as far as we are aware, they have not been
fully investigated. Last but not least, even though our focus is on enumerations and not on
probabilistic results, we show that our enumerative results (recurrences, generating functions,
and explicit expressions) can be straightforwardly extended to deal with probabilistic runs,
when assuming bitstrings generated by independent and identically distributed Bernoulli
random variables.




                                                2
1.1    Structure of the Paper
This paper is structured as follows. In Section 2 we study the number of binary strings of
length n that contain prescribed quantities of runs of ones under different constraints. In
Section 3 we consider the probabilistic extensions of the most relevant among the Section 2
results. We then consider in Section 4 the same problems as in Section 2, but when the
binary strings are constrained to having fixed Hamming weight. Sections 5, 6 and 7 address
the basic same problems as Sections 2, 3 and 4, respectively, when runs of ones and runs of
zeros are jointly considered —rather than only runs of ones.
In Section 8 we study the total number of runs (under different constraints) that are found
over all binary strings, or over certain subsets of these. In Section 9 we essentially address
the same problems as in Section 8, but when the goal is instead to count the number of ones
contained in runs of ones, rather than the runs themselves. Finally, we draw the conclusions
of this work in Section 10.


1.2    Definitions
We start by precisely stating the definition of “run” that we use, to avoid any possible
confusion with previous works.

Definition 1.1 (Run, Length of a Run). A run in a binary string is an uninterrupted
sequence of bits of the same kind, flanked on each side either by the opposite bit or by the
start/end of the string (Mood’s counting criterion [44]). The number of bits of the same
kind in a run is its length.

Definition 1.2 (n-String). To minimise excessive wording, a binary string of length n is
referred to in the following as an n-string.

Definition 1.3 (k-Run). A k-run means a run of length k, where k ≥ 0.

Definition 1.4 (Null Run, Nonnull Run). A null run is defined to be a 0-run, i.e., a zero-
length run, whereas a nonnull run is a k-run with k ≥ 1.

Remark 1. Observe that with the definition above we are just explicitly identifying the
existence of zero-length runs. Although perhaps initially puzzling, null runs (0-runs) follow
directly from Mood’s criterion in Definition 1.1. Letting b̃ = mod(b + 1, 2) with b ∈ {0, 1},
a null run of b s in an n-string occurs whenever a b̃ is immediately followed by another b̃,
                                                              ′ ′  ′
or whenever the n-string starts/ends with b̃. For example, 0 010 contains three 0-runs of
      ′                                      ′ ′ ′ ′ ′
ones, 0101 contains one 0-run of ones, and 0 0 0 0 contains five 0-runs of ones, where the
apostrophes mark the positions of the null runs of ones. Even if stating the obvious, observe
that a null run of b s contains no b s. The apparent lack of applications for null runs is
probably the reason why they have not been considered by previous authors. However, we


                                              3
will see that disregarding null runs is not an option in a complete theory of runs, as they
play a fundamental role in key theoretical and practical results.                         □



Definition 1.5 ((k ≤ k)-Run, (≥ k)-Run, (≤ k)-Run). A (k ≤ k)-run is defined to be a
k-run with 0 ≤ k ≤ k ≤ k. One may verbalise a (k ≤ k)-run by saying a “lower k to higher k
run”. A (≥ k)-run is defined to be a (k ≤ ∞)-run, which may include null runs, whereas
a (≤ k)-run is defined to be a (1 ≤ k)-run if null runs are excluded and a (0 ≤ k)-run
otherwise. These additional definitions can be naturally verbalised as a “greater or equal
than k run” and a “smaller or equal than k run,” respectively. Lastly, observe that we can
also express a k-run as a (k ≤ k)-run.

Definition 1.6 (Odd and Even Runs, p-Parity Runs). An odd (even) run is defined to be a
run whose length is odd (even). More generally, we use the expression odd (even) runs when
describing ensembles of runs whose lengths, which may be different, are all odd (even). For
the sake of brevity in mathematical expressions, we also use the term p-parity run, which
refers to an odd run when p = 1, and to an even run when p = 0. Throughout the paper we
only consider nonnull even runs —odd runs are always nonnull.

Remark 2. Mood’s criterion is perhaps the most common way to define a run. However,
among the authors who adhere to this criterion, some use the term “run” to denote runs
formed by one single kind of bit —typically, runs of ones— whereas others jointly consider
runs of ones and zeros. With the first convention, ‘11000111100’ contains two runs of ones:
a 2-run and a 4-run (or two runs of zeros: a 3-run and a 2-run), whereas with the second
one it contains four runs (of ones and zeros): two 2-runs, one 3-run and one 4-run. In
the first group of authors we would find most of those who deal with success runs, such as
Apostol [2], Balakrishnan and Koutras [5], Makri et al. [38] and many others. Among authors
simultaneously considering runs of ones and zeros we have Wishart and Hirschfeld [69],
Stevens [63], Wald and Wolfowitz [66] —in their classic runs-based test—, Bloom [7], again
Balakrishnan and Koutras [5], and others. The combination of Mood’s criterion with two
kinds of runs in binary strings is summarised in the following statement by Feller [18, p. 42]:
“In any ordered sequence of elements of two kinds, each maximal subsequence of elements of
like kind is called a run”. It should be noted, though, that Feller also considers runs of ones
(success runs) separately from runs of zeros, and that he often handles other definitions of
“run”—see below.
As just mentioned, the reader must be aware that the literature contains definitions of “run”
divergent from Mood’s criterion. A relevant case for this paper is the definition used by
Flajolet and Sedgewick [19, 58] or by Nyblom [49], for whom a run of ones of length k
in a binary string still refers to the appearance of k consecutive ones, but not necessarily
terminated by a zero or by the end of the string (or started by a zero or by the start of the
string). Nevertheless, when discussing n-strings devoid of such runs, as these authors do,
this definition of a run of ones of length k is completely equivalent to a (≥ k)-run of ones
according to our definition above.

                                              4
Other authors consider not only definitions of “run” which do not conform to Mood’s cri-
terion, but also enumeration schemes that count overlapping runs. As a relevant example,
Balakrishnan and Koutras consider Type I, II and III run enumeration schemes in their trea-
tise on runs [5], of which only type II is relevant to the criterion adopted in this paper. To
illustrate these schemes, consider the string ‘110111001111101’. With these authors’ Type I
scheme —originally proposed by Feller [18, p. 305]— a k-run does not have to end with
the opposite bit or with the end of the string. Thus, according to this scheme, we may say
that the aforementioned string contains four runs of ones of length 2: ‘110111001111101’,
which is not compatible with Mood’s criterion. However, Feller’s and Mood’s criteria are
equivalent when considering the longest run. Feller’s criterion, which has often been used
in runs theory, is somewhat unnatural. In fact, it was only introduced by this author to
be able to study runs through renewal theory, as he considered that the classical theory of
runs —based on Mood’s criterion— was “messy”. Next, using the Type II scheme in [5]
we may say that the aforementioned string contains three runs of ones of length at least 2:
‘110111001111101’, which is compatible with Mood’s criterion, and equivalent to us saying
that it contains three (≥ 2)-runs of ones, or, equivalently, three (2 ≤ 15)-runs of ones. On
the other hand, overlapping of runs is allowed with the Type III scheme handled by Bal-
akrishnan and Koutras (Ling’s criterion [35]), according to which we may say, for example,
that the string in question contains seven runs of ones of length 2: ‘110111001111101’. Of
course, this is not compatible with our criterion either.
Finally, different authors have used names other than “run” to describe runs that —usually
either avant la lettre or unwittingly— comply with Mood’s criterion. Some examples are: se-
quence [24], join [69], group [63], block [3, 26], subset of consecutive ones [2], isolated tuple [2],
or clump [7]. Runs with particular lengths have also been called singles/singletons/isolated
letters/isolated ones [8, 2, 24, 3] (i.e., 1-runs), isolated pairs [2] (i.e, 2-runs), isolated triples [2]
(i.e., 3-runs), etc. The expression maximal block/run [26, 36] has also been used to denote a
run that follows Mood’s criterion, but we believe that this term is best avoided in order to pre-
vent any misunderstandings with maximum length runs. Like other previous authors [20, 60],
we are crediting Mood in Definition 1.1, but the fact is that the use of the term “run” with
the exact same meaning precedes him (see for example [13]), and the first explicit statement
of “Mood’s criterion” seems to have been subsequently done by Mosteller [45, p. 229]. Looser
uses of the term “run” in probability are even older [50].                                               □

                                                    5
Remark 3. For the sake of brevity, in the remainder of this paper the unqualified term
“run” always implicitly refers to a run of ones unless explicitly specified otherwise. Most of
the exceptions to the aforementioned naming convention occur in Sections 5, 6, 7, and 8.4,
where we jointly consider runs of ones and runs of zeros, and where we explicitly indicate the
nature of the runs as required. Likewise, we assume that runs may be null unless explicitly
indicated otherwise.                                                                         □




2     Number of n-Strings that Contain Prescribed Quan-
      tities of Runs Under Different Constraints
In this section we address several fundamental enumeration problems that involve count-
ing the number of n-strings that contain prescribed quantities of runs subject to different
constraints.


2.1    Number of n-Strings that Contain Exactly m (k ≤ k)-Runs
We denote by wk≤k (n, m) the number of n-strings that contain exactly m (k ≤ k)-runs, i.e.,
m runs whose lengths are within k and k. Notice that such n-strings may also contain other
runs longer than k or shorter than k. Because of the general nature of the definition of a
(k ≤ k)-run, the results established in this section form the foundation that allows us to
address many other runs-related enumerations in a simple —and sometimes even trivial—
way.
We begin by formulating a necessary condition that underpins our approach.

Necessary Condition 1. (Existence of n-strings containing m (k ≤ k)-runs)
                                                            
                                                        n+1
                   wk≤k (n, m) > 0 =⇒ 0 ≤ m ≤                  .                           (1)
                                                        k+1
                                                                                             □




The reason for the nonnegativity of m is clear, and perhaps only the case m = 0 merits
some explanation: observe that we can have n-strings devoid of (k ≤ k)-runs. As for the
upper bound in (1), if we have m runs with lengths k1 , . . . , km such that k ≤ kj ≤ k for
j = 1, . . . , m, then, because the number of zeros in an n-string that contains m runs must
be at least m − 1, a necessary condition for these runs to fit in an n-string is
                                      m k + m − 1 ≤ n,                                     (2)
which is equivalent to the upper bound in (1). Condition (1) is not sufficient, as the validity
of its right-hand side does not guarantee that we can accommodate m (k ≤ k)-runs within an

                                              6
n-string. However, if the right-hand side is not true, then such accommodation is certainly
impossible.
In order to streamline some formulas, it is convenient to name the difference between both
sides of inequality (2):
                                 e = n − (m k + m − 1).                                (3)
Of course, e ≥ 0 is another way to express the upper bound in (1).

Remark 4. Necessary condition (1) does not hold if n < −1. However, consider the two
cases
                                n = −1 and n = 0,                                 (4)
for which (1) does not negate the possibility of existence of n-strings containing m (k ≤ k)-
runs, as long as m = 0. Even if the two cases above may seem absurd —especially n = −1,
since n = 0 is commonly accepted to be the degenerate case of an empty string— they play
the crucial role of initialising all the recurrences that we give in this section —and also in
Sections 3 and 4.                                                                            □




Finally, we also discuss a relevant enumeration that stems from the main one studied in this
section: the number of n-strings that contain at least m (k ≤ k)-runs, which we denote by
  ≥m

2.1.1   Recurrences

Let us first obtain a recurrence relation for wk≤k (n, m). Consider the contribution to
wk≤k (n, m) from the ensemble of n-strings that begin with an i-run. If k ≤ i ≤ k then
they contribute wk≤k (n − (i + 1), m − 1) to wk≤k (n, m). On the other hand, if i > k or
i < k then they contribute wk≤k (n − (i + 1), m) to wk≤k (n, m). Observe that the “+1” in
(i + 1) is there to guarantee that the first bit immediately after the i-run is a zero —i.e.,



                                                  7
to terminate the run. Thus, considering all possible lengths of a starting run we obtain the
following bivariate recurrence:
                          k                                   k−1
                          X                             X                     
          wk≤k (n, m) =         wk≤k n − (i + 1), m − 1 +  wk≤k n − (i + 1), m
                          i=k                                 i=0
                              n
                              X                          
                          +           wk≤k n − (i + 1), m .                                (7)
                              i=k+1

The natural question is: what if i ≥ n? As we see later, as long as we take (1) into account,
we do not have to worry about such questions with the correct initialisation of the recurrence.
An alternative way to find a recurrence for wk≤k (n, m) is based on the following observation:
if we remove the first bit of all n-strings, then we can see that wk≤k (n, m) is approximately
equal to 2 wk≤k (n − 1, m). The overcounting or undercounting in this estimate with respect
to the true enumeration only depends on the cases in which an n-string starts with a k-run
or with a (k + 1)-run, as these are the only cases in which the number of (k ≤ k)-runs can
be increased or decreased with respect to the (n − 1)-string formed by removing the first
bit. Thus, in order to make the aforementioned estimate exact we just need to make the
following adjustments to it:

  a) For every n-string that starts with a k-run, we must add one whenever the remaining
     (n − (k + 1))-string contains (m − 1) (k ≤ k)-runs, but we must subtract one whenever
     the remaining (n − (k + 1))-string contains m (k ≤ k)-runs already.
  b) For every n-string that starts with a (k+1)-run, we must subtract one from the estimate
     whenever the remaining (n − (k + 2))-string contains (m − 1) (k ≤ k)-runs, but we
     must add one whenever the remaining (n − (k + 2))-string contains m (k ≤ k)-runs .

Collecting the contributions from a) and b) we get the following alternative recurrence:
                                                                                    
  wk≤k (n, m) = 2 wk≤k (n − 1, m) + wk≤k n − (k + 1), m − 1 − wk≤k n − (k + 1), m
                                                                                    
                                  − wk≤k n − (k + 2), m − 1 + wk≤k n − (k + 2), m . (8)

Observe that recurrence (8) can also be derived from recurrence (7) using wk≤k (n, m) −
wk≤k (n − 1, m). Recurrence relation (8) is equivalent to but simpler than (7): the number
of recursive calls in (7) is n (full-history recurrence), whereas the number of recursive calls
in (8) is five, independently of n. Also, unlike (7) which contains an n-dependent summation,
recurrence (8) is directly amenable to the computation of the generating function associated
to wk≤k (n, m), as we see in Section 2.1.2. In the remainder we work with (8).
To initialise (8) we can use the case n = 1, in which we have by inspection that

2.1.2   Generating Functions

Let us next obtain the bivariate ordinary generating function (ogf) associated to wk≤k (n, m),
i.e.,                                    XX
                           Wk≤k (x, y) =        wk≤k (n, m) xn y m .                      (14)
                                           n   m

Notice that we have not set summation limits in (14), which means that we are adding over
all integers n and m. This is because, in order to streamline our task, we follow Graham
et al.’s procedure in [27, Sec. 7.3] and apply (14) to a version of recurrence (8) valid for all
values of n and m. First of all, taking necessary condition (1) into account, recurrence (8)
is valid not only for n ≥ 1 and m ≥ 0, but also for n < −1 and/or m < 0 —in which cases
wk≤k (n, m) = 0. So let us see what happens in the remaining two cases n = −1 and n = 0,
by computing wk≤k (−1, m) and wk≤k (0, m) using (8) and then comparing the results with
the correct initialisation values in (11) and (12).

                                               9
In the first case, from (8) and (1) we mistakenly have that wk≤k (−1, m) = 0 instead of the
correct value wk≤k (−1, m) = Jm = 0K, but we can “fix” this by adding Jn = −1KJm = 0K
to (8). In the second case, according to (8) and using (11) and (1) we wrongly have that
wk≤k (0, m) = 2 Jm = 0K when k > 0 and wk≤k (0, m) = Jm = 0K + Jm = 1K when k = 0,
rather than the correct value (12). Once again, we can “fix” these cases just by subtracting
Jn = 0KJm = 0K from (8). Therefore an extended version of recurrence (8) valid for all values
of n and m when taking (1) into account is

2.1.3   Explicit Expressions

It is possible, in principle, to get explicit expressions for wk≤k (n, m) from (17) —and for
  m
wk≤k  (n) from (18)— using the same standard method that we adopt in many subsequent
sections. This method is, essentially, the repeated application of the (negative) binomial
theorem, plus, in some cases, the solution of one or more simple Diophantine equations.
However, the reader may verify that, in this general case, this strategy leads to overcompli-
cated expressions, which depend on whether gcd(k − k + 1, k) is equal to or greater than
one. Consequently, we only obtain explicit expressions for the special cases of wk≤k (n, m)
considered in Sections 2.2–2.7.


2.2     Number of n-Strings that Contain Exactly m k-Runs
We denote the number of n-strings that contain exactly m k-runs by wk (n, m). Notice that
such n-strings may also contain other runs of lengths different than k.
The earliest reference that we know of for this enumeration is the work of Apostol [2], who
gave several recurrences and a generating function for wk (n, m) motivated by an electri-
cal engineering problem. This relevant piece of work appears to have faded into obscurity,
perhaps due to its idiosyncratic naming conventions. Koutras and Papastavridis [33] recov-
ered some of Apostol’s results, but most later authors were unaware of them. They also
were unaware of the first closed-form formula for wk (n, m) —a triple-summation expres-
sion obtained as a special case of a more general computation— given by Magliveras and
Wei [37, Thm. 2.3]. Sinha and Sinha [60] produced a triple-summation explicit expression for
wk (n, m), drawing on a generating function for the interstitial gaps between the m k-runs.
Soon afterwards, Makri and Psillakis [40] gave a simpler double-summation explicit formula
for wk (n, m). Their derivation exploited an existing combinatorial result of their own for the
distribution of balls in urns [39]. More recently, Madden [36] has given a generating function
for the equivalent problem of enumerating the n-strings that begin with zero and contain a
prescribed number of runs of a given length.
As far as we are concerned, this problem is a special case of the enumeration in Section 2.1
with k = k = k, and thus
                                  wk (n, m) = wk≤k (n, m).                              (19)



                                              11
We also study the number of n-strings that contain at least m k-runs, which from (19)
and (5) is
                                         ⌊X
                                          k+1 ⌋
                                          n+1

                                ≥m
                              wk (n) =          wk (n, t).                       (20)
                                                t=m

                                                                               
                                     − wk n − (k + 2), m − 1 + wk n − (k + 2), m ,           (22)

which, from (11) and (12), are both initialised by

                                 wk (−1, m) = Jm = 0K,                                       (23)
                                              q          y
                                  wk (0, m) = m = Jk = 0K .                                  (24)

Remark 5. Apostol gives a host of recurrences for this problem [2, Thms. 2–11]. In Apostol’s
              (k)
notation, Am = wk (n, m), but he also uses Sm (n) = w1 (n, m), Pm (n) = w2 (n, m) and
Tm (n) = w3 (n, m). This author gives several recurrences for enumerating the n-strings with
prescribed numbers of isolated singletons (i.e., 1-runs): in particular, he gives recurrences
for w1 (n, 0) with n ≥ 3, for w1 (n, 1) with n ≥ 3, for w1 (n, 2) with n ≥ 3, and for w1 (n, m)
with n ≥ 3 and m ≥ 2. He then gives recurrences for enumerating n-strings with prescribed
numbers of isolated pairs (i.e., 2-runs), in particular, recurrences for w2 (n, 0) with n ≥ 3, for
w2 (n, 1) with n ≥ 3, for w2 (n, 2) with n ≥ 3, and for w2 (n, m) with n ≥ 3 and m ≥ 2. Finally,
he extends these recurrences to isolated k-tuples (i.e., k-runs). He first gives a recurrence for
wk (n, 0) with n ≥ k + 2, then a recurrence for wk (n, 1) with n ≥ k + 2, lastly a recurrence
for wk (n, m) with m ≥ 2, k ≥ 1 and n ≥ mk + m − 1 [in our notation, e ≥ 0, see (3)].
Any enumeration that might be obtained through Apostol’s various recurrences can also be
obtained through either (21) or (22) in a simpler and more general way—our recurrences
above are valid for all n ≥ 1, m ≥ 0 and k ≥ 0 when taking (1) into account. All this comes
down to the necessity of considering the cases n = −1 and n = 0 in the initialisation —see
related comments at the very end of Section 10. The importance of the initial values (23)
and (24) cannot be understated: with them, all of Apostol’s recurrences would become a
special case of (22).

                                               12
To conclude this remark, in the specific case of m = 0 recurrence (22) becomes
                                                                             
           wk (n, 0) = 2 wk (n − 1, 0) − wk n − (k + 1), 0 + wk n − (k + 2), 0 ,        (25)

i.e., we recover the same recurrence given by Madden in [36, Case 4]—note that wk (n, 0) =
N (n + 1, k + 1) in this author’s notation. See further comments about Madden’s work in
Remark 6. Of course, (25) is also one of Apostol’s recurrences [2, Eq. (15)].            □

Remark 6. The ogf (27) was first given by Apostol [2, Eq. (25)], who determined it through
his aforementioned recurrences. Koutras and Papastavridis [33, Sec. 5.c] also found (27)
through the particularisation of a general result based on occupancy models (i.e., distribu-
tions of balls into cells). Finally, this ogf is also Madden’s main theorem in [36]. Bearing
in mind that —except for [60]— all prior work on this problem was unbeknownst to him,
Madden observes regarding (27): “This result is quite elementary, but we have not been able
to find it in any other source”. Rediscoveries and reworkings like this are not uncommon in
the long history of the theory of runs, and we will find more as the paper progresses. In any
case, they show that fundamental problems concerning runs still merit a closer look.        □

Equation (30) determines a single value of t for any given (r, s) pair. So, as long as we
guarantee that
            r
               t ≥ 0, we only need to focus our attention on r and s. Because s ≤ r (as
otherwise s = 0), we have from (30) that t ≤ e − r. So, the highest value of r for which t

Expression (31) is similar to the one previously derived by Makri and Psillakis [40, Eq. (8)],
which they obtained as a corollary to an earlier probabilistic result of theirs [39]. Like their
expression, (31) is simpler than the expressions given by Magliveras and Wei [37, Thm.
2.3] —the earliest explicit result— or by Sinha and Sinha [60, (3)], because it involves two
summations rather than three.

Remark 7. Concurring with Madden’s comments in [36, p. 2], we also found that Sinha
and Sinha’s expression for wk (n, m) [60, Eq. (3)] (in their notation, Nnm,k ) is not always in
agreement with the true counts. Their expression for Nnm,k is essentially correct, but it fails
when e = 0 [in our notation, see (3)]. One can fix this minor oversight by starting the
summations in [60, Eqs. (2) and (3)] at r = 0 rather than at r = 1.                           □

2.3    Number of n-Strings that Contain Exactly m (≥ k)-Runs
Let us next address the enumeration of the n-strings that contain exactly m (≥ k)-runs (i.e.,
runs of length k or longer), which we denote by w≥k (n, m). Notice that such n-strings may
also contain other runs shorter than k.
Explicit solutions to this problem were previously given by Muselli [46] —in the context of
probabilistic success runs— and by Makri and Psillakis [40]. In the special case m = 0, which
is of particular relevance in Section 2.6, a generating function and an explicit expression were
given by Sedgewick and Flajolet [57], and a recurrence by Nyblom [49]. A probabilistic recur-
rence and a probability generating function were also given by Balakrishnan and Koutras [5]
—see comments about these results in Remark 21 (Section 3.3).
If k > n then w≥k (n, m) = 0, and so we assume k ≤ n. Therefore, the problem is a special
case of the enumeration in Section 2.1 with k = k and k = n, and thus

                                  w≥k (n, m) = wk≤n (n, m).                                (32)

                                              15
We also study the number of n-strings that contain at least m (≥ k)-runs, which from (32)
and (5), is

                                                  t=m

though these two generating functions are different, they have the same coefficients in their
power series expansion for n ≥ 1.                                                                   □

2.3.3   Explicit Expression

To obtain an explicit expression for w≥k (n, m) we first apply in succession the negative
binomial theorem and the binomial theorem to the denominator of (39). With this expansion
we can express (39) as
                                   X m + r X r
            m
          [y ]W≥k (x, y) = (1 − x)                     (−1)(r−s) 2s xn−e+(k+1)r−ks , (42)
                                   r≥0
                                         r     s≥0
                                                    s

where we have used (3) in the exponent of x. In order to determine the coefficient of xn
in the above expression we have to find the values of indices r and s that solve each of the
following two Diophantine equations:

                                      (k + 1)r − ks = e,                                        (43)
                                      (k + 1)r − ks = e − 1.                                    (44)

Since gcd(k + 1, −k) = 1, we can solve both equations. By inspection, a particular solution
to (43) is r = s = e. Thus the general solution is of the form r = e − k t and s = e − (k + 1) t

                                                 17
for integer t, which fulfils s ≤ r for nonnegative t. As we also need to guarantee r ≥ 0
and s ≥ 0, the valid range of t is 0 ≤ t ≤ ⌊e/(k + 1)⌋. Similarly, a particular solution
to (44) is r = s = e − 1, and thus the general solution is of the form r = e − 1 − k t and
s = e − 1 − (k + 1) t for integer t, and the range of t is 0 ≤ t ≤ ⌊(e − 1)/(k + 1)⌋.
Combining these solutions we can see that the coefficient of xn in (42), or, equivalently,
[xn y m ]W≥k (x, y), is

                                                     2    e − 1 − kt      e − 1 − (k + 1)t

Observe that we use just one summation on t, rather than the difference of two summations
on t with different ranges, by taking advantage of the fact that the last binomial coefficient
becomes zero for t > ⌊(e−1)/(k+1)⌋. This single-summation formula is clearly different from
the previous double-summation formulas given by Muselli [46, Thm. 1 with p = q = 12 gives
w≥k (n, m)/2n ] and Makri and Psillakis [40, Eq. (23)] —which closely resemble each other,
and which were derived through combinatorial analysis. However Muselli also simplified his
double-summation expression into a single-summation formula comparable to (45) [46, Thm.
3].
The case m = 0 is especially relevant, as we see in more detail in Section 2.6 —see also
Remark 10 below. With this argument choice, expression (45) becomes

Remark 10. The special case w≥k (n, 0) with k ≥ 1 can also be used to enumerate the n-
strings that feature their first consecutive appearance of k ones at index i, which we denote
by wbk (n, i). Notice that we are not only referring to the first appearance of a k-run: for
the avoidance of doubt, index i marks the position of the kth one in the first uninterrupted
sequence of k ones, which may be followed by a zero, a one, or the end of the string. To
obtain wbk (n, i) we just have to make the following observation: if the first k consecutive ones
occur at index i, then the initial (i − (k + 1))-substring must be devoid of (≥ k)-runs. For
every such start of the n-string we have 2n−i possible endings. Combining these two facts,
we thus have that
                           wbk (n, i) = w≥k (i − (k + 1), 0) 2n−i Ji ≤ nK.                   (50)
Note that this formula works even if i < k, in which case w≥k (i − (k + 1), m) = 0 through
necessary condition (1). The enumeration w  bk (n, i) is relevant in waiting-time type problems.
Bearing in mind (47), the reader is referred to [5, Sec. 2.8] for a literature review on the role
of jth order Fibonacci numbers in the context of waiting-time problems with runs.               □

                                          20
2.4      Number of n-Strings that Contain Exactly m Nonnull (≤ k)-
         Runs
Let us next address what is essentially the counterpart of the problem in the previous section:
the enumeration of the n-strings containing exactly m nonnull (≤ k)-runs (i.e., runs of
length k or shorter, but strictly greater than zero), which we denote by w≤k (n, m). Hence,
in this section we assume k ≥ 1. Notice that the n-strings that we enumerate may also
contain any number of null runs, and/or of runs with lengths longer than k.
Austin and Guy [3] gave a recurrence and a semi-explicit expression for “the number of
binary sequences of length n in which the ones occur only in blocks of length at least k”
(where k ≥ 2), that is to say, for the special case w≤(k−1) (n, 0) of the enumeration that we
consider in this section. Other than in [3], w≤k (n, m) seems not to have received any attention
in the literature —which is somewhat surprising, given that this enumeration looks like a
mere variation of w≥k (n, m). A plausible reason for this is that it may be harder to address
this problem through direct combinatorial analysis. This hypothesis is suggested by the
explicit expression (60) for w≤k (n, m) that we derive later —a triple-summation expression,
as opposed to the single sum expression (45) for w≥k (n, m). Another possible explanation
might be the perception that the problem in this section has fewer applications. Whatever
the cause, at least one good reason for studying w≤k (n, m) is that the special case w≤k (n, 0)
plays a key role in Section 2.7, just like we have mentioned that w≥k (n, 0) plays a key role
in Section 2.6.

k = 1 and k = k, and thus
                                w≤k (n, m) = w1≤k (n, m).                             (51)

and (5), is

We now derive an explicit expression for w≤k (n, m) by finding [xn y n ]W≤k (x, y), or, equiva-

Collecting the exponents of x, we see that we have to determine next the ranges of the
summation indices r, s, t and p that fulfil 2m − 1 + k r + s + (k + 2) t + p = n, or, using (3),
k r + s + (k + 2) t + p = e. Let us define at this point the auxiliary variable
                                              d = e − k r.
As all indices are nonnegative, s + (k + 2) t + p ≥ 0. So we must guarantee d ≥ 0 in
order to have solutions to our problem, which implies r ≤ ⌊e/k⌋. We next have to solve
d = s + (k + 2) t + p for integers s, t and p, where s, t, p ≥ 0. The maximum of t happens
when s = p = 0, and thus t ≤ ⌊d/(k + 2)⌋. Given t, the maximum of s happens when p = 0,
and thus s ≤ d − (k + 2) t. Finally, given t and s, we have that p = d − (k + 2) t − s.
Collecting all these solutions, we can see that the coefficient of xn in (58) is
                ⌊e/k⌋      ⌊d/(k+2)⌋

This expression also constitutes an alternative to the semi-explicit expression w≤(k−1) (n, 0) =
round(ck γkn ) given by Austin and Guy [3, p. 85], which is based on asymptotic considerations
and requires determining a special constant ck and finding the real root γk of a kth order
polynomial.

2.5    Number of n-Strings that Contain Exactly m Nonnull Runs
In this section we study the number of n-strings that contain exactly m nonnull runs (of
arbitrary lengths, all strictly greater than zero), which we denote by w(n, m). As may be
expected, this problem is also a special case of the enumeration in Section 2.1 with k = 1
and k = n, and thus
                                    w(n, m) = w1≤n (n, m).                             (62)
Alternatively, using the enumerations in Sections 2.3 and 2.4 we may also write

                             w(n, m) = w≥1 (n, m) = w≤n (n, m).                        (63)

We also study the number of n-strings that contain at least m runs, which from (62) and (5)
is

2.6    Number of n-Strings Whose Longest Run Is a k-Run or a
       (≤ k)-Run
We denote the number of n-strings whose longest run is a k-run by wk (n), whereas w≤k (n)
represents the number of n-strings whose longest run is a (≤ k)-run. A recurrence for w≤k (n)
was previously given by Schilling [54], and, in the context of probabilistic success runs,
explicit expressions were given by Godbole [23] and Muselli [46]. Finally, Flajolet and
Sedgewick [19] gave a generating function for w≤k (n) constructed through the symbolic
method, and Prodinger gave its asymptotic behaviour [52]. As for previous work on wk (n),
probabilistic versions of this enumeration were given by Muselli [46] and by Makri et al. [38],
and Schilling provided asymptotic estimates of its distribution [55].
For all k ≥ 0, the two enumerations that we are interested in are related as follows
                                                            k
                                                            X
                                                w≤k (n) =         wj (n).                                        (71)
                                                            j=0

Conversely, for k ≥ 1
                                        wk (n) = w≤k (n) − w≤(k−1) (n),                                          (72)
whereas w0 (n) = w≤0 (n). Both enumerations follow directly from the results in Section 2.3
because of the following fact:
                                 w≤k (n) = w≥(k+1) (n, 0).                             (73)
Thus, w0 (n) = w≥1 (n, 0) = 1 for n ≥ 1, as the longest run is a null run only in the all-zeros
n-string.

                                                        27
Of course, considering (73) and (32), w≤k (n) can also be expressed as a special case of
wk≤k (n, m) in Section 2.1, but using (73) we are able to simplify our presentation and
directly obtain explicit expressions.


2.6.1   Recurrences

We start by finding recurrences for w≤k (n). Schilling’s recurrence [54, Eq. (1)] is recovered
by directly applying (73) to (34):
                                              k
                                              X
                               w≤k (n) =            w≤k (n − (i + 1)).                         (74)
                                              i=0

On the other hand, an alternative recurrence is obtained by directly applying (73) to (35),
which gives
                      w≤k (n) = 2 w≤k (n − 1) − w≤k (n − (k + 2)).                     (75)
From (73), (36) and (37), we can see that the initialisation of both (74) and (75) is
                                     w≤k (−1) = w≤k (0) = 1.                                   (76)
Remark 13. Schilling initialised (74) in [54, Eq. (1)] using
                                      w≤k (n) = 2n       if n ≤ k,                             (77)
but there is no need to resort to (77) when initialising (74) with (76). Also, Bloom gave
recurrence (75) for k = 4 [8, Eq. (9)].                                                 □



We may also obtain recurrences for wk (n) for k ≥ 1 by relying on (74) and (75). Using (74)
in (72), after some elementary algebraic manipulations we get
                               k−1

                    wk (n) =         wk (n − (i + 1)) +          wj (n − (k + 1)),             (78)

                                        k−1

From (72) and (76), it follows that wk (−1) = wk (0) = 0 for k ≥ 1. As seen from (78)
and (79), we also need initialisation for k = 0. As w0 (n) = w≥1 (n, 0), we have from (36)
and (37) that w0 (−1) = w0 (0) = 1. Thus, the initialisation of recurrences (78) and (79) is
                                 wk (−1) = wk (0) = Jk = 0K.
The ease with which these recurrences are initialised shows that null runs are inherent to
this problem —although the stronger reason for taking null runs into account will be seen
in Section 5.

2.6.3   Explicit Expressions

Explicit expressions for wk (n) and w≤k (n) are available through (72), (73) and (46). The
resulting formula for w≤k (n) is comparable to the simplified single-summation combinatorial
expression given by Muselli [46, Cor. 1 using p = q = 1/2 gives w≤(k−1) (n)/2n ]. In con-
trast, our single-summation expression for wk (n) is much simpler than the triple-summation
expression given by Makri et al. [38, Thm. 2.1.4 using p = q = 1/2 gives wk (n)/2n ].

2.7      Number of n-Strings Whose Shortest Nonnull Run Is a k-Run
         or a (≥ k)-Run
Let us call wk (n) the number of n-strings whose shortest nonnull run is a k-run, and
let w≥k (n) be the number of n-strings whose shortest run is a (≥ k)-run. We therefore


                                               30
assume k ≥ 1 in this section. These problems have been studied by Makri et al. [38] in the
context of probabilistic success runs.
Both quantities are related as follows:
                                                      n
                                                      X
                                          w≥k (n) =          wj (n),
                                                       j=k


and so we also have
                                   wk (n) = w≥k (n) − w≥(k+1) (n).                                   (86)
The enumeration of w≥k (n) and that of wk (n) follow directly from the results in Section 2.4,
because for k > 1 we have the following relation:

                                    w≥k (n) = w≤(k−1) (n, 0) − 1,                                    (87)

whereas
                                            w≥1 (n) = 2n − 1.                                        (88)
We subtract one both in (87) and in (88) to discount the all-zeros n-string. Of course,
considering (51), we have that the connection in (87) can also be expressed using the general
enumeration wk≤k (n, m) studied in Section 2.1, but (87) allows us to simplify our presentation
and to obtain explicit expressions.


2.7.1     Recurrences

From (87) and (53), a recurrence for w≥k (n) when k > 1 is
                                            n
                                            X
              w≥k (n) = w≥k (n − 1) +             w≥k (n − (i + 1)) + max(n − k + 1, 0),             (89)
                                            i=k

and from (87) and (54) an alternative recurrence for k > 1 is

             w≥k (n) = 2 w≥k (n − 1) − w≥k (n − 2) + w≥k (n − (k + 1)) + Jk ≤ nK.                    (90)

Considering (87) and (55), the initialisation of these two recurrences is

                                      w≥k (−1) = w≥k (0) = 0.                                        (91)

From (89) and (90) we may obtain recurrences for wk (n) when k > 1. Using (89) in (86) the
first recurrence is
                                  n
                                  X                             n
                                                                X
          wk (n) = wk (n − 1) +           wk (n − (i + 1)) +           wj (n − (k + 1)) + Jk ≤ nK,   (92)
                                  i=k+1                         j=k


                                                     31
where we have used max(n − k + 1, 0) − max(n − k, 0) = Jk ≤ nK. Using (90) in (86) we get
the alternative recurrence
                                       n
                                       X                          n
                                                                  X

                                       j=k                        j=k+1
                                                                                                 (93)
where we have used Jk ≤ nK − Jk + 1 ≤ nK = Jk = nK. Inputting (91) in (86), we see that
both (92) and (93) are initialised by
                                     wk (−1) = wk (0) = 0.
Finally, we address the k = 1 case. From (86) and (88) we obtain w1 (n) = 2n − 1 − w≥2 (n),
which can be evaluated using recurrences (89) or (90).

Remark 16. The same strategy in (86) was used by Makri et al. [38, Thm. 2.1.2] to get, in
their notation, P (Mn = k) from an explicit computation for P (Mn ≥ k). These two prob-
abilities correspond to the enumerative quantities wk (n) and w≥k (n). Also, recurrence (89)
is the exact enumerative counterpart of the probabilistic recurrence given by Makri et al.
for P (Mn ≥ k) [38, Thm. 2.1.3]. These authors derived their recurrence from their ex-
plicit expression, rather than the other way around, and they did not provide initialisation
values.                                                                                    □

2.8      Number of n-Strings that Contain Exactly m Nonnull p-Parity
         Runs
Let w[p] (n, m) represent the number of n-strings that contain exactly m nonnull p-parity
runs, i.e., exactly m runs whose lengths k1 , . . . , km are strictly greater than zero and have
parity mod(ki , 2) = p for i = 1, . . . , m. The n-strings that we enumerate may have more

                                                  33
than m nonnull runs, as long as the lengths of all additional runs have parity opposite to p,
and they may also have any number of null runs. For the first time in this paper we address
an enumeration unrelated to the results in Section 2.1. To the best of our knowledge, the
only authors that have addressed a similar problem are Grimaldi and Heubach [28], who
studied the number of n-strings devoid of odd runs —a special case of w[p] (n, m) with p = 1
and m = 0.
First of all, we state a necessary condition similar to (1).

Necessary Condition 2. (Existence of n-strings containing m nonnull p-parity runs)
                                                                
                                                        n+1
                  w[p] (n, m) > 0 =⇒ 0 ≤ m ≤                       .               (98)
                                                     2 + Jp = 0K
                                                                                                □




This is really the same condition as (1) in Section 2.1, just noting that the lengths of all
nonnull p-parity runs are lower bounded by k = 1 + Jp = 0K.
We also study the number of n-strings that contain at least m nonnull p-parity runs, which
               m
we denote by w[p] (n). This enumeration can be obtained from w[p] (n, m) as

                                                 ⌊ 2+Jp=0K
                                                     n+1
                                                           ⌋
                                  ≥m
                                                    X
                                 w[p] (n) =                    w[p] (n, t).                  (99)
                                                    t=m



2.8.1   Recurrences

We can produce a recurrence to enumerate w[p] (n, m) with a similar strategy as in Sec-
tion 2.1.1. Since we are not counting null runs, we can split the quantity w[p] (n, m) into two
contributions:

  a) The n-strings that begin with 0 contribute w[p] (n − 1, m) to w[p] (n, m).

  b) As for the n-strings that begin with 1, those that start with an odd i-run contribute
     w[p] (n − (i + 1), m − Jp = 1K) to w[p] (n, m), whereas those that start with an even
     nonnull i-run contribute w[p] (n − (i + 1), m − Jp = 0K). Equivalently, the n-strings that
     start with a nonnull i-run contribute w[p] (n − (i + 1), m − Jp = mod(i, 2)K) to w[p] (n, m).

Collecting these two contributions we get the following recurrence:
                                          n
                                          X                                          
        w[p] (n, m) = w[p] (n − 1, m) +         w[p] n − (i + 1), m − Jp = mod(i, 2)K .     (100)
                                          i=1

To find initial values for recurrence (100) we consider the case n = 1, in which we know by
inspection that
                       w[p] (1, m) = 2 Jp = 0KJm = 0K + Jp = 1KJm = 1K.                (101)
On the other hand, setting n = 1 in (100) and taking into account (98) yields

                       w[p] (1, m) = w[p] (0, m) + w[p] (−1, m − Jp = 1K).              (102)

We wish (102) to equal (101). Taking into account (98), equality between the two expressions
is achieved by choosing
                            w[p] (−1, m) = w[p] (0, m) = Jm = 0K,                      (103)
which are therefore the initial values of (100).

all n and m, which we use subsequently.

We can obtain one by calculating w[p] (n, m) − w[p] (n − 2, m) using the extended recurrence.

    w[p] (n, m) − w[p] (n − 2, m) = w[p] (n − 1, m) − w[p] (n − 3, m)

Remark 17. Unlike in Sections 2.1.1 and 2.1.2, obtaining the above difference before mak-
ing (100) valid for all n and m does not render a valid recurrence. In Section 2.1.1 we were
able to first separately reason each recurrence and then verify that the second one could be

                                               35
obtained from the first, but this is not the case here. In general, we can freely operate with
a recurrence to obtain a new equivalent recursive relation as long as the original recurrence
is valid for all integer values of its arguments. If this is not true, then the manipulation is
not guaranteed to render a valid relation.                                                    □

3     Extensions to Probabilistic Runs
At several points in Section 2 we have specialised probabilistic results about success runs
from other authors (e.g., [46, 38]) in order to compare them to our enumerations. Thus,
one might conclude that the enumerative results that we have given so far are less general
than their probabilistic counterparts. But this is a two-way street: as we see in this section,
every enumerative recurrence in Section 2 also has a direct probabilistic translation into the
case in which the n-strings are outcomes from n independent and identically distributed
(iid) Bernoulli random variables with parameter q, i.e., when each bit in the n-string is
independently drawn with probability 0 < q < 1 of getting a 1.
Since probabilistic results are not our main goal in this paper, we mainly examine how to
extend our most relevant enumerative results in Sections 2.1 and 2.8 to the probabilistic
scenario described above. Observe that this also includes the probabilistic extensions of
our results in Sections 2.2–2.7, which are, essentially, special cases or consequences of Sec-
tion 2.1. In fact, we also look into the probabilistic extension of the enumerative results
in Sections 2.2, 2.3 and 2.6, since these are connected with several prior findings in the
literature.
In order not to overload notation, it is understood that all recurrences, probability generating
functions, explicit expressions, and moments in this section implicitly depend on q.


3.1    Probability that an n-String Contains Exactly m (k ≤ k)-Runs
We call πk≤k (n, m) the probability that an n-string contains exactly m (k ≤ k)-runs. The
obvious case is q = 1/2, in which πk≤k (n, m) = wk≤k (n, m)/2n . Likewise, the probability
                                                      ≥m         ≥m
that an n-string contains at least m (k ≤ k)-runs is πk≤k (n) = wk≤k (n)/2n in this case. We
examine next how to get these quantities for arbitrary q.

                                              38
3.1.1   Recurrences

We may directly write two equivalent probability recurrences for πk≤k (n, m) by using the two
enumerative recurrences in Section 2.1.1 in conjunction with the law of total probabilities.

Remark 20. As a rule, pgf (123) allows us to pursue explicit expressions for πk≤k (n, m) in
particular cases of k and k, in the same way that we have sought explicit expressions for

                                                40
wk≤k (n, m) in particular cases of k and k in Sections 2.2–2.5. If we are interested instead in
the computation of moments for a given n, we can do so through (122) but sometimes the
alternative route through [xn ]Πk≤k (x, y) is cleaner. Although getting the coefficient of xn is
not as straightforward as getting the coefficient of y m (it cannot usually be done in closed
form), in special cases one can still get manageable expressions for [xn ]Πk≤k (x, y) through the
binomial theorem. The extra work involved sometimes actually pays off, as neater explicit
expressions can sometimes be determined through [xn ]Πk≤k (x, y).                               □

In this section we obtain an explicit expression for πk (n, m) by extracting the coefficient of

us see that s ≤ e − kt, whereas r is determined by the previous equation for any two values

The expectation (137) was originally given by Marbe [42, 43] and then put on a sounder
footing by Cochran [13, Eq. (5)] —see more details in Section 8, Remark 35. The expectation
and variance of M0,n were implicitly given by Bloom [7] —see discussion towards the end of
Remark 27 in Section 5.


3.3     Probability that an n-String Contains Exactly m (≥ k)-Runs
We denote by π≥k (n, m) the probability that an n-string contains exactly m (≥ k)-runs.
This is a special case of the probability in Section 3.1 with k = k and k = n, and thus

                                       π≥k (n, m) = πk≤n (n, m).                                (142)

We discuss this special case in some detail because several authors have previously studied
this probability [17, 16, 65, 46, 5].

3.3.4   Moments

The pgf (153) can also be used to obtain moments of the rv modelling the number of (≥ k)-
runs in an n-string drawn at random, which we denote by M≥k,n . With standard probabilistic
notation,                                    
                                Pr M≥k,n = m = π≥k (n, m).                          (155)
The expectation of this random variable is E(M≥k,n ) = (d/dy)[xn ]Π≥k (x, y)|y=1 . After dif-
ferentiating (153) with respect to y, setting y = 1 lets us see that the only nonzero term
corresponds to t = 1, as long as k ≤ n. Therefore,

                        E(M≥k,n ) = (n − k)(1 − q) + 1 q k Jk ≤ nK.
                                                        
                                                                                       (156)

The second factorial moment is E(M≥k,n (M≥k,n − 1)) = (d2 /dy 2 )[xn ]Π≥k (x, y)|y=1 . After
differentiating (153) twice with respect to y and then setting y = 1, we see that the only
nonzero term corresponds to t = 2. Thus, the second factorial moment is zero if k > 0 and
⌊(n + 1)/k⌋ = 1. Therefore we have that

E M≥k,n (M≥k,n −1) = (n−2k)(1−q)+1+q (n−2k)(1−q)q 2k Jk > 0KJk < nK+Jk = 0K .
                                                                                        

                                                                                       (157)
The variance of M≥k,n follows from the two moments above —see Section 3.2.3. The expec-
tation (156) was apparently first given by Goldstein [25, Eq. (5)], without using a pgf. Aki
and Hirano also obtained (156) and its corresponding variance [1, pp. 317–318] through a
pgf similar to (153) [1, Eq. (5)].

Remark 21. Krishnan Nair [47, p. 84] gave a pgf which should be equivalent to (149),
but which appears to be incorrect. Also, Balakrishnan and Koutras gave a recurrence for

                                              46
π≥k (n, m) very similar to (143) [5, p. 144] but with different initialisation. These authors also
gave a bivariate pgf for this case [5, Eq. (5.11)], which, however, they did not get from their
aforementioned recurrence. In our notation, the pgf given by Balakrishnan and Koutras is
                                               1 + (y − 1)(q x)k
                          Π≥k (x, y) =                                  .                   (158)
                                         1 − x − (y − 1)(1 − q)q k xk+1
It is easier to obtain [y m ]Π≥k (x, y) in closed-form from (148) than from (158) —(149) is
witness to this. A consequence is that is simpler obtaining (151) using (148) than using (158).
                                                                                                □



Remark 22. The results in this section also allow us to recover the oldest historical result
on success runs by de Moivre [17, Prob. LXXXVIII, pp. 243–248]: “To find the Probability
of throwing a Chance assigned a given number of times without intermission, in any given
                                                                   ≥1
number of Trials,” or, in terms of our definitions, to find π≥k       (n) = 1 − π≥k (n, 0). Using
the same numerical values originally employed by de Moivre and (154), for n = 10, k = 3
                                    ≥1
and q = 1/2, we may see that π≥3       (10) = 0.5078 = 65/128, which we can also verify using
  ≥1       10                                               ≥1
w≥3 (10)/2 —see (33)—, whereas if q = 2/3 then π≥3             (10) = 0.8121 = 592/729. The
truncated decimal values are computed using (154), whereas the fractions can be obtained,
                                      ≥1
for instance, by determining [x10 ]π≥3   (x) using (150) and a computer algebra system. Both
probabilities are correctly given in [17], even though there is a known mistake in the procedure
followed therein [30, p. 418]. De Moivre did not give a recurrence nor did he provide a proof
for his solution, which was based on adding a given number of terms in the power series
expansion of a generating function. According to Hald’s account [30, p. 420], the first
solution to de Moivre’s problem based on a finite difference equation (i.e., a recurrence) was
given by Simpson. Simpson also gave an explicit solution as an infinite series. However,
as also indicated by Hald, Laplace was the first author who essentially derived pgf (151)
for m = 1 [16, p. 252] —see Remark 23 below. Finally, Uspensky gave a semi-explicit
                                                                 ≥1
closed-form expression for the probability π≥k (n, 0) = 1 − π≥k     (n) [65, Eq. (3)].          □

3.4     Probability that the Longest Run of an n-String Is a (≤ k)-Run
We denote the probability that the longest run of an n-string is a (≤ k)-run by π ≤k (n). Of
course, this is a special case of the results in the previous section because

                                    π ≤k (n) = π≥(k+1) (n, 0).                           (162)

We treat this special case separately because it was previously dealt with by Muselli [46]
and Schilling [54].

3.4.3   Explicit Expressions

As w≤k (n, r) has the single-summation closed form (213), Schilling would probably be pleased
to see that (165) is actually a double-summation explicit expression for π ≤k (n) —although
his asymptotic results are a more powerful proposition indeed. In any case, from (162)
and (154) we get a single-summation explicit expression for π ≤k (n), similar to the one given
by Muselli [46, Eq. (16)]. Equivalent formulas were previously found in reliability problems
by Hwang [32, Thm. 3] and by Lambiris and Papastavridis [34, Eq. (1)].

where we have used (173). This pgf gives the probability that a binary string contains at
least m nonnull p-parity runs.


3.5.3    Explicit Expressions

We next determine explicit expressions for π[p] (n, m) by extracting the coefficient of xn
in (173). We begin with the case p = 1, where we first express (173) as [y m ]Π[1] (x, y) =

3.5.4   Moments

We denote by M[p],n the random variable that models the number of p-parity runs in an
n-string drawn at random, i.e.,

                                    Pr(M[p],n = m) = π[p] (n, m).                        (180)

Using (172), we may obtain the moments of M[p],n . First, the expectation is E(M[p],n ) =
[xn ](∂/∂y)Π[p] (x, y)|y=1 . Since we have that

by applying the negative binomial theorem twice we get

Focussing on the p = 1 case, the coefficient of xn is found using the nonnegative indices t

                              E(M[0],n ) = E(M≥1,n ) − E(M[1],n ).                    (184)
The second factorial moment may be computed in a similar fashion.

                                                 52
3.6    Discussion
It should be clear from our exposition in this section that it is relatively straightforward
to obtain probabilistic counterparts of every aspect of the enumeration problems that we
have studied in Section 2, and that this procedure is generally simpler and more versatile
than addressing success runs from a combinatorial analysis viewpoint. In particular, pgfs are
easy to obtain through recurrences, and they are generally preferable to complicated explicit
expressions when it comes to obtaining moments. If desired, explicit expressions can be also
obtained from pgfs, and, importantly, they are also a conduit to asymptotic probabilistic
results.
Last but not least, we see later in Section 4.5 that all the probabilistic results in this section
can also be approximated through deterministic enumerations via the law of large numbers.


4     Number of n-Strings of Hamming Weight r that Con-
      tain Prescribed Quantities of Runs Under Different
      Constraints
In this section we address the same basic problems as in Section 2 but when the n-strings
are restricted to having Hamming weight r —i.e., to containing exactly r ones. In the termi-
nology of Goulden and Jackson [26, Sec. 2.4.7] these are enumerations with type restriction.
Many early works in the theory of runs assume this constraint, particularly those that use
runs for hypothesis tests. The results in this section are complemented by other results given
later in Section 7, where runs of ones and zeros are jointly considered.
We cover the counterparts of the problems in Sections 2.1 and 2.8 in Sections 4.1 and 4.4,
respectively. The former enumeration also solves the same problems as in Sections 2.2–2.7
when the fixed Hamming weight constraint is observed. We actually discuss two of these
special cases in more detail in Sections 4.2 and 4.3, since they have been studied by a
considerable number of previous authors [63, 44, 45, 6, 9, 51, 54, 56].
Whereas the enumerative recurrences in Sections 2.1 and 2.8 are bivariate, solving fixed
Hamming weight versions of these enumerations involves establishing trivariate recurrence
relations. This means that the corresponding ogfs are also trivariate, which in some scenarios
may hinder the determination of compact explicit expressions. Nevertheless, this is possible
in a number of special cases.




                                               53
4.1     Number of n-Strings of Hamming Weight r that Contain Ex-
        actly m (k ≤ k)-Runs
Let wk≤k (n, m, r) represent the number of n-strings of Hamming weight r that contain exactly
m (k ≤ k)-runs. As usual, the n-strings that we enumerate may have other runs, as long
as they are longer than k or shorter than k. Observe that this is a conditional    version of
                                                                      n
                                                                        
the general enumeration in Section 2.1 in which we only consider the r n-strings that have
Hamming weight r.
Next, we need to tighten necessary condition (1) in order to take the Hamming weight
constraint into account.

Necessary Condition 3. (Existence of n-strings of Hamming weight r containing m (k ≤
k)-runs)
                                                            !
                                                       n+1      r
             wk≤k (n, m, r) > 0 =⇒ 0 ≤ m ≤ min               ,       .         (185)
                                                       k+1     k
Observe that if r > 0 and k = 0 then the upper bound on m is n + 1. When r = 0 and k = 0
the ratio r/k is undefined, but the upper bound is still n + 1, as this is the number of null
runs in the all-zeros n-string.                                                            □




4.1.1   Recurrences

We can resort to a nearly identical reasoning as in Section 2.1.1 to get a recurrence for this
enumeration: the quantity wk≤k (n, m, r) can be broken down into different contributions
corresponding to the ensemble of n-strings that start with an i-run. If i > k or i < k then
the contribution is wk≤k (n − (i + 1), m, r − i), but if k ≤ i ≤ k then the contribution is
wk≤k (n − (i + 1), m − 1, r − i). Thus, considering all possible lengths of the starting run we
get the following trivariate recurrence:
                      k                                          k−1
                      X                                    X                           
   wk≤k (n, m, r) =         wk≤k n − (i + 1), m − 1, r − i +  wk≤k n − (i + 1), m, r − i
                      i=k                                        i=0
                          n
                          X                                 
                      +           wk≤k n − (i + 1), m, r − i .                           (186)
                          i=k+1

Observe that the only difference with respect to recurrence (7) are the updates of the Ham-
ming weights in the recursive invocations on the right hand side. Following the same steps
as in Section 2.1.1, we can argue an alternative recurrence equivalent to (186):

wk≤k (n, m, r) = wk≤k (n − 1, m, r) + wk≤k (n − 1, m, r − 1)
                                                                              

                                                   54

wk≤k (n − 1, m, r − 1) using (186).
In the following we work with the simpler recurrence (187), as it is free from n-dependent
summations and thus directly amenable to determining the ogf. In order to find initialisation
values we rely on the case n = 1, in which we know by inspection that
                                                           
  wk≤k (1, m, r) = Jr = 0K Jm = 0KJk ≥ 1K + Jm = 2KJk = 0K

4.2     Number of n-Strings of Hamming Weight r that Contain Ex-
        actly m Nonnull Runs
We denote by w(n, m, r) the number of n-strings of Hamming weight r that contain ex-
actly m nonnull runs. This enumeration —in fact, the probability of drawing m nonnull
runs when n-strings of Hamming weight r are drawn uniformly at random, which we denote
by π(n, m, r) = w(n, m, r)/ nr — was explicitly solved by a number of authors [63, 44, 10, 56].
This is a particular case of the analysis in Section 4.1 with k = 1 and k = n, and therefore

                                 w(n, m, r) = w1≤n (n, m, r).                            (194)


4.2.1   Recurrences

Specialising recurrences (186) and (187) to this case yields the following two recursive rela-
tions:
                                               n

                        w(−1, m, r) = w(0, m, r) = Jm = 0KJr = 0K.                       (197)

Thus the coefficient of xn z r corresponds to the indices that fulfil n = s + t + 2m − 1 and

Either in this enumerative form or in its probabilistic version π(n, m, r) = w(n, m, r)/ nr ,

expression (201) was previously given by Stevens [63, Eq. (3.05)], Mood [44, Eq. (2.11)],
Charalambides [10, p. 97], Gibbons and Chakraborti [22, Cor. 3.2.1] Schuster [56, Cor. 3.5]
and others. Schuster explicitly notes that π(n, m, r) is a hypergeometric distribution.


4.3     Number of n-Strings of Hamming Weight r Whose Longest
        Run Is a k-Run or a (≤ k)-Run
We consider here the enumeration of the n-strings of Hamming weight r whose longest run
is a k-run or a (≤ k)-run, which we respectively denote by wk (n, r) and w≤k (n, r). These
are the Hamming weight constrained versions of the enumerations in Section 2.6.
Many authors have studied these problems, both from enumerative and probabilistic view-
points. In the latter case we refer to the situation in which the n-strings of Hamming
weight r are drawn uniformly     at random, where we are interested in the probabilities
π k (n, r) = wk (n, r)/ r and π ≤k (n, r) = w≤k (n, r)/ nr —i.e., the cumulative probability.
                        n
                         

The earliest study is by Mosteller, who gave π ≥k (n, r) = 1 − π ≤(k−1) (n, r) for the case


                                                57
n = 2r [45, Eq. (4)]. Bateman [6] first considered an arbitrary ratio r/n in the same prob-
lem and gave an explicit formula for π ≥k (n, r). David and Barton [15], Burr and Cane [9],
Philippou and Makri [51], Gibbons and Chakraborti [22], and Schuster [56] gave exact for-
mulas for π ≤k (n, r). Recurrences for w≤k (n, r) and π ≤k (n, r) where given by Schilling [54]
and Schuster [56], respectively. Finally, and Nej and Reddy [48] gave a recurrence and a
closed-form explicit expression for wk (n, r).
We address this enumeration using the exact same approach as in Section 2.6. Like in that
section, we first state the connection between wk (n, r) and w≤k (n, r). For all k ≥ 0 we have
that
                                                k
                                                X
                                   w≤k (n, r) =    wj (n, r),                            (202)
                                                       j=0

and, conversely, for all k ≥ 1 it holds that
                                 wk (n, r) = w≤k (n, r) − w≤(k−1) (n, r),                     (203)
whereas w0 (n, r) = w≤0 (n, r). The expression that allows us to get these two quantities
through the general enumeration wk≤k (n, m, r) is

                                     w≤k (n, r) = w≥(k+1) (n, 0, r),                          (204)
where w≥k (n, m, r) = wk≤n (n, m, r). Thus w0 (n, r) = w≥1 (n, 0, r) = Jr = 0K for n ≥ 1, as
the longest run is a null run only in the all-zeros n-string, which can only happen if r = 0.


4.3.1   Recurrences

We first find recurrences for w≤k (n, r). By applying (204) to (186) we get
                                             k
                                             X
                            w≤k (n, r) =           w≤k (n − (i + 1), r − i),                  (205)
                                             i=0

which parallels (74). Also, by applying (204) to (187) we get the following alternative
recurrence:
                                                                                   
    w≤k (n, r) = w≤k (n − 1, r) + w≤k (n − 1, r − 1) − w≤k n − (k + 2), r − (k + 1) , (206)
which parallels (75). From (204), (190) and (191), both (205) and (206) are initialised using
                                  w≤k (−1, r) = w≤k (0, r) = Jr = 0K.                         (207)
We can also get recurrences for wk (n, r) when k ≥ 1. By inputting (205) in (203) we find
the recurrence
                           k−1
                           X                                 k
                                                             X
             wk (n, r) =         wk (n − (i + 1), r − i) +         wj (n − (k + 1), r − k),   (208)
                           i=0                               j=0


                                                     58
whereas if we input (206) in (203) we get the alternative recurrence

                                      Xk−1                   Xk
wk (n, r) = wk (n−1, r)+wk (n−1, r−1)+     wj (n−(k+1), r−k)−   wj (n−(k+2), r−(k+1)).
                                           j=0                       j=0
                                                                                        (209)
From (203) and (207), the initialisation of both recurrences is wk (−1, r) = wk (0, r) = 0 for
k ≥ 1, but we can see that we also need initialisation for the case k = 0. Since w0 (n, r) =
w≥1 (n, 0, r), from (190) and (191) we have that w0 (−1, r) = w0 (0, r) = Jr = 0K. Thus
recurrences (208) and (209) are both initialised by

                            wk (−1, r) = wk (0, r) = Jk = 0KJr = 0K.

Again, (208) and (209) parallel their unconstrained counterparts (78) and (79), respectively.

Remark 25. Recurrence (205) was given by Schilling in [54, Eq. (4)]. This author examines
in some detail the case k = 3 of (205), but is nevertheless somewhat vague       about its general
initialisation. Schuster also gave a recurrence for π ≤k (n, r) = w≤k (n, r)/ nr based on Pascal’s
triangle of order k + 1 [56, Cor. 4.3], which is essentially different from either (205) or (206).
In any case, both recurrences above are simpler. Also, recurrence (208) was previously given
by Nej and Reddy [48, Thm. 3.2] —in their notation, wk (n, r) = Fn (r, k). These authors
state that their recurrence is valid for n ≥ 3, 1 ≤ r ≤ n − 2 and ⌊n/(r + 1)⌋ ≤ k ≤ n.
Regarding the piecemeal validity of the recurrences given by previous authors mentioned in
this remark: notice that all the recurrences given here are valid for all n ≥ 1 and r, k ≥ 0
with the initialisations given, just taking necessary condition (185) into account. As in other
such fragmentary recurrences we have met before (see Section 2.3), this shows the necessity
of considering the cases n = −1 and n = 0 in the initialisation in order to obtain the simplest
and most general recurrences.                                                                 □

Considering (204), we may obtain the ogf W≤k (x, z) = n,r w≤k (n, r) xn z r by setting k =
