4.3.3   Explicit Expressions

We find next an explicit expression for w≤k (n, r), for which we rewrite (210) first as W≤k (x, z) =
                                  −1
x−1 1 − (x − xk+2 z k+1 )/(1 − xz) . Applying the negative binomial theorem twice and then
the binomial theorem to this expression, we can expand the ogf as
                              X X X s + t − 1 t 
              W≤k (x, z) =                                 (−1)p (xz)p(k+1)+s xt−1 .         (212)
                              t≥0 s≥0 p≥0
                                            t−1          p

To extract the coefficient of xn z r we have to find nonnegative indices t, s and p that fulfil
                                   n = p(k + 1) + s + t − 1,
                                   r = p(k + 1) + s.
From the second equation, the maximum of p happens when s = 0, and thus p ≤ ⌊r/(k + 1)⌋.
One value of p determines s = r − p(k + 1) and t = n + 1 − p(k + 1) − s. We thus have
from (212) that w≤k (n, r) = [xn z r ]W ≤k (x, z) is
                                     r
                                  ⌊ k+1 ⌋                      
                                   X        n − p(k + 1) n − r + 1
                                            p
                     w≤k (n, r) =     (−1)                          .                       (213)
                                  p=0
                                               n −  r        p

Bateman was the first author who gave an expression parallel to (213) [6, p. 101]. Her formula
gives the probability π ≥k (n, r), and it is based on a generating function for compositions
restricted to a maximum part —see start of Section 5 for the connection between runs and
compositions. David and Barton [15, p. 230] and then Schuster [56, Cor. 4.2] gave alternative
derivations of (213) in the form π ≤k (n, r). The former authors used combinatorial arguments
and characteristic (Bernoulli) random variables, whereas the latter author used the theory
of exchangeability of random variables and generating functions. Schuster was driven by the
computational impracticality of some earlier explicit expressions for π ≤k (n, r) [9, 51], but he
must have missed the expression by Bateman that we mentioned above.
From (213) we also get a single-summation explicit expression for wk (n, r) through (203).
Regarding this case, Nej and Reddy found a more compact closed-form expression (without
summations) for wk (n, r) through combinatorial analysis, although restricted to the special
case r < n − 1 and r < 2k [48, Thm. 3.1]. This suggests that it might be possible to
evaluate (213) in closed form, perhaps using the “snake oil” method [68], although we have
not succeeded in doing so.

                                                60
4.4     Number of n-Strings of Hamming Weight r that Contain Ex-
        actly m Nonnull p-Parity Runs
In this section we consider the counterpart of Section 2.8 under a Hamming weight constraint.
We denote by w[p] (n, m, r) the number of n-strings of Hamming weight r that contain exactly
m nonnull p-parity runs.
As usual, we need to state first a necessary condition similar to (185).

Necessary Condition 4. (Existence of n-strings of Hamming weight r containing m nonnull
p-parity runs)
                                                                        !
                                                  n+1               r
       w[p] (n, m, r) > 0 =⇒ 0 ≤ m ≤ min                    ,                 .   (214)
                                               2 + Jp = 0K     1 + Jp = 0K

                                                                                                     □




4.4.1   Recurrence

We obtain a recurrence with a rationale similar to that in Section 2.8.1: n-strings that start
with 0 contribute w[p] (n − 1, m, r) to w[p] (n, m, r); on the other hand, n-strings that start
with a nonnull i-run contribute w[p] (n − (i + 1), m, r − i) if p = mod(i, 2) but w[p] (n − (i +
1), m − 1, r − i) if p ̸= mod(i, 2). These two contributions lead to the following trivariate
recurrence:
                                          n

                                          i=1

4.5      Probabilistic Connections
If the probability of drawing a one is q, then an n-string drawn at random will roughly contain
nq ones for large n with high probability —by the law of large numbers. Thus, for large n,
all the probabilistic results in Section 3 can be approximated through their deterministic
counterparts in this section, by assuming that the Hamming weight of an n-string drawn at
random is always ⌊nq⌋. With this assumption we have that, for large n,
                                                                     −1
                                                             n
                            πk≤k (n, m) ≈ wk≤k (n, m, ⌊nq⌋)                    ,              (222)
                                                            ⌊nq⌋

and                                                                 −1
                                                               n
                              π[p] (n, m) ≈ w[p] (n, m, ⌊nq⌋)              .                  (223)
                                                              ⌊nq⌋
Of course, these connections may also be used in reverse, that is to say, to approximate
Hamming-constrained enumerations using probabilistic expressions.




                                                  62
5     Number of n-Strings that Contain Prescribed Quan-
      tities of Nonnull Runs of Ones and/or Zeros Under
      Different Constraints
In Section 2 we studied enumerations of n-strings containing prescribed numbers of runs of
ones in binary strings. In this section we see how analogous techniques allow us to enumerate
the n-strings that contain prescribed quantities of runs of ones and/or zeros —i.e., for the
sake of clarity, the case where each run may indistinctly be a run of ones or a run of zeros—
in several similar scenarios. We focus on extending the main enumerations in Sections 2.1
and 2.8 to the same scenarios for runs of ones and zeros, but we also examine the extension of
the special enumerations in Sections 2.2, 2.5 and 2.6, as these problems have been addressed
by other authors [8, 26, 54, 64]. The main change in our approach in this section with
respect to previous sections is that we always establish two mutual recurrences, rather than
a single one —as we have always done up to this point. These mutual recurrences correspond,
respectively, to enumerations of n-strings that start with a zero or with a one. Importantly,
we only consider nonnull runs of ones or zeros.
As mentioned in the introduction, a number of authors have considered runs of ones and runs
of zeros jointly [63, 66, 18, 26, 54, 7, 8, 5]. On first impression, this setting is not so relevant
to the study of success runs, but, in contrast, it can lead to better statistics in runs-based
hypothesis tests (as more information is taken into account when considering both kinds
of runs). Importantly, it also has clear direct implications for the problem of compositions
(ordered partitions) of n, which are the different ways in which we can partition the sum
1 + 1 + · · · + 1 = n into nonzero ordered parts. The intimate connection between runs of ones
and zeros in n-strings and compositions is easy to understand: we can visualise the parts of
a composition of n as the ordered lengths of an alternating series of runs of ones and zeros in
an n-string, and in its ones’ complement. For example, consider the following composition
of n = 9 into five parts: 1 + 3 + 2 + 2 + 1 = 9. We can represent this composition using
the sequence of lengths of five alternating runs of ones and zeros in two related 9-strings:
‘011100110’, and its ones’ complement, ‘100011001’ —see also Figure 1, where the top row
can be interpreted as a partition of 1+1+· · ·+1 into nonzero parts. Two other consequences
of this observation are: 1) all enumerations in this section must be even valued, and 2) the
aforementioned mutual recurrences are always symmetric. To the best of our knowledge, the
earliest author who saw a connection between compositions and runs was Bateman [6].
The relationship between runs of ones and compositions is perhaps less transparent. How-
ever, the results in Section 2 also hint at a close association between runs of ones and
compositions, as shown by the many OEIS sequences cited that are simultaneously related
to both. Grimaldi and Heubach [28] have in fact explicitly described one case of this rela-
tionship. The reason for these connections is the intrinsic link that exists in certain scenarios
between the enumerations of (n − 1)-strings containing prescribed quantities of runs of ones
and the enumerations of n-strings containing prescribed quantities of runs of ones and/or

                                                63
zeros —which, as we have discussed in the previous paragraph, are themselves directly con-
nected to compositions of n. We explicitly give the simplest of such links in Theorems 5.1
and 5.2 in Sections 5.1 and 5.5, respectively.
Remark 30 at end of this section overviews the main consequences of our results for the
problem of compositions.


5.1     Number of n-Strings that Contain Exactly m Nonnull (k ≤ k)-
        Runs of Ones and/or Zeros
We denote by sk≤k (n, m) the number of n-strings that contain exactly m nonnull (k ≤ k)-runs
of ones and/or zeros, which may also contain other runs with lengths not in the prescribed
range. If we denote the number of compositions of n that contain exactly m parts between k
and k by ck≤k (n, m), then, from the observations in the previous section, we also have that
                                                          1
                                      ck≤k (n, m) =         s (n, m).                                      (224)
                                                          2 k≤k
As in Section 2.1, we establish first a necessary condition that guides our enumeration.

Necessary Condition 5. (Existence of n-strings containing m nonnull (k ≤ k)-runs of ones
and/or zeros)                                             
                                                          n
                      sk≤k (n, m) > 0 =⇒ 0 ≤ m ≤             .                     (225)
                                                          k
                                                                                                              □



The upper bound in (225) is due to the fact that it must always hold that m k ≤ n for an
n-string to be potentially able to hold m nonnull (k ≤ k)-runs of ones and/or zeros. Thus,
unlike in the enumerations of runs of ones, we now necessarily have that the enumerations
must be zero when n = −1, which implies that only the case n = 0 is involved in the
initialisations.

We can also produce this theorem by comparing recurrences (7) and (231) together with
their initialisations, but any possible hesitation about the relationship between these two
families of enumerations vanishes after comparing their respective ogfs. As far as we know,
the earliest sign of Theorem 5.1 appears in a result by Schilling —see start of Section 5.4
Theorem 5.1 is the main reason why we have restricted our analysis in this section (Sec-
tion 5) to nonnull runs of ones and zeros. Since null runs of ones are well defined (see again
Definition 1.4 and Remark 1 in the introduction), results for nonnull runs of ones and zeros
can always be translated into results for runs of ones through (235) when k ≥ 1.

Remark 26. A Smirnov word is a string of symbols from an alphabet in which no two
adjacent symbols are equal —equivalently, a string without levels [26, Secs. 2.4.13, 2.4.14].
The number of binary Smirnov words of length n is trivially two for n ≥ 1. We verify next
that this can indeed be seen through (235). Using this expression and (39) we can write
                                                                            1−x

5.2.3   Explicit Expression

Remark 27. Bloom used the term single to mean a 1-run of ones or zeros —a name remi-
niscent of Apostol’s isolated singleton for a 1-run of ones [2]— and obtained a recurrence for

is valid for all n and m thanks to the inhomogeneous term —absent in Bloom’s expression.

in any of its five instances in (242), which the author deals with through the initialisation

obtains, through counting arguments, a single-summation explicit expression for s1 (n, m).

5.4    Number of n-Strings Whose Longest Nonnull Run Is a (≤ k)-
       Run of Ones or Zeros
We denote by s≤k (n) the number of n-strings whose longest nonnull run is a (≤ k)-run of
ones or zeros. Thus, we assume k ≥ 1. We can study this problem as a special case of the
results in Section 5.1, because

                                   s≤k (n) = s(k+1)≤n (n, 0).                            (254)

Again, we look at this special case in some detail because previous authors studied this
problem. Schilling deduced through simple reasoning that [54, Eq. (2)]

                                 s≤k (n) = 2 w≤(k−1) (n − 1),                            (255)

which is Theorem 5.1 in action in this special case. Also, Suman studied the number of b-ary
strings of length n whose longest run is a (≤ k)-run of any of the b-ary symbols [64], giving a
recurrence, an ogf, and three explicit expressions (one of them asymptotic). We only consider
here Suman’s nonasymptotic results in the binary case, in which his r and k correspond in
our setting to k and 2, respectively. In Suman’s notation, s≤k (n) = cn . Finally, Bloom also
considered the asymptotics of s≤k (n) in a particular case [8].

is also Schilling’s recurrence (74) transformed by (255).

Suman extracted a double-summation explicit expression for s≤k (n) from (259) [64, Thm. 1],

                                               71
5.5     Number of n-Strings that Contain Exactly m Nonnull p-Parity
        Runs of Ones and/or Zeros
We denote by s[p] (n, m) the number of n-strings that contain exactly m nonnull p-parity
runs of ones and/or zeros, i.e., exactly m runs of ones and/or zeros whose lengths k1 , . . . , km
have parity mod(ki , 2) = p for i = 1, . . . , m and are strictly greater than zero. The n-strings
that we enumerate may have more than m nonnull runs of ones and/or zeros, as long as the
lengths of all these additional runs have parity opposite to p, and any number of null runs
(whose parity is even). For the same reason as in Section 5.1, if we call c[p] (n, m) the number
of compositions of n that contain exactly m “p-parity parts” —i.e., parts whose parity is p—
then we also have that
                                                   1
                                    c[p] (n, m) = s[p] (n, m).                               (260)
                                                   2
As far as we are aware, Goulden and Jackson are the only authors who have addressed
enumeration problems related to the one considered in this section. These authors have
enumerated the n-strings in which an odd run of zeros is never followed by an odd run of
ones [26, Sec. 2.4.6], and the n-strings in which all runs of ones are even and all runs of
zeros are odd [26, Ex. 2.4.3]. Clearly, the results in this section do not encompass these two
enumerations.
Before continuing, we state a necessary condition similar to (225).

Necessary Condition 6. (Existence of n-strings containing m nonnull p-parity runs of ones
and/or zeros)                                                   
                                                          n
                  s[p] (n, m) > 0 =⇒ 0 ≤ m ≤                       .                (261)
                                                     1 + Jp = 0K
                                                                                                □




This is really the same necessary condition as (225), just noting that the lengths of all nonnull
p-parity runs of ones and/or zeros are lower bounded by k = 1 + Jp = 0K.


5.5.1   Recurrence

We can produce a recurrence to enumerate s[p] (n, m) with the same strategy used in Sec-
tion 5.1: we first produce two mutual recurrences for the number of n-strings that start with
bit b and contain exactly m p-parity runs of ones and/or zeros, denoted by sb[p] (n, m), where
b ∈ {0, 1}. Considering all the n-strings that start with a nonnull i-run of each length, we
can readily see through a similar reasoning as in Section 2.8.1 that the mutual recurrences
sought are given by
                                        n
                                        X
                       sb[p] (n, m) =         sb̃[p] (n − i, m − Jp = mod(i, 2)K),          (262)
                                        i=1


                                                     72
for b ∈ {0, 1}, and where b̃ = mod(b + 1, 2). To find initial values for the mutual recurrences
in (262) we use the case n = 1, in which we know by inspection that
                        sb[p] (1, m) = Jm = 0KJp = 0K + Jm = 1KJp = 1K.                      (263)
On the other hand, setting n = 1 in (262) yields

                                sb[p] (1, m) = sb̃[p] (0, m − Jp = 1K).                      (264)
We may verify that (264) equals (263) when
                                        sb[p] (0, m) = Jm = 0K.                              (265)
which are therefore the initial values of the mutual recurrences in (262). Since the enumer-

                               s[p] (n, m) = s0[p] (n, m) + s1[p] (n, m),                    (266)

5.5.2    Generating Functions

We now obtain the ogf S[p] (x, y) = n,m s[p] (n, m) xn y m . First of all, we make s[p] (n, m)
                                      P
valid for all values of n and m. Setting n = 0 in (267) we get s[p] (0, m) = 0 instead of the
correct value 2Jm = 0K. Thus we just need to add 2Jm = 0KJn = 0K to (267) to obtain an
extended recurrence valid for all n and m, which we use subsequently.
Next, we obtain a recurrence without an n-dependent summation by determining s[p] (n, m)−
s[p] (n − 2, m) using the extended recurrence. This yields
        s[p] (n, m) = s[p] (n − 2, m) + s[p] (n − 1, m − Jp = 1K) + s[p] (n − 2, m − Jp = 0K)
                                                        
                      + 2Jm = 0K Jn = 0K − Jn = 2K .                                          (269)
By multiplying next (269) on both sides by xn y m and then adding on n and m we find that
                                                      2 (1 − x2 )
                           S[p] (x, y) =                                  .                 (270)
                                           1 − x y Jp=1K − x2 1 + y Jp=0K
In general, this ogf cannot always be connected to its counterpart (106) that counts only
runs of ones, unlike Sk≤k (x, y) in the previous section —see Theorem 5.1. But not all hope
is lost, as the next simple theorem shows.

                                                     73
Theorem 5.2. Fundamental link between enumerations of n-strings that contain prescribed
quantities of nonnull even runs of ones and/or zeros and enumerations of n-strings contain-
ing prescribed quantities of odd runs of ones.

                                     S[0] (x, y) = 2x W[1] (x, y).                        (271)

Proof. Substitute (106) into (271).                                                           □



Remark 29. There is no similar converse mapping between S[1] (x, y) and W[0] (x, y), as one
might expect. This asymmetry is rather unsatisfying, but it has a satisfying explanation:
we have only considered nonnull p-parity runs of ones to get W[p] (x, y). This assumption is
immaterial when p = 1, as odd runs of ones are always nonnull. However, when p = 0 we are
disregarding all null runs of ones, which have even parity. Thus, the symmetry of the setting
with respect to runs of ones and zeros is broken. We will not delve further into this issue
here, but it is not difficult to see that if we modify W[p] (x, y) to include null runs of ones,
then symmetry is restored and (271) becomes S[p] (x, y) = 2x W[p̃] (x, y) with p ∈ {0, 1} and
p̃ = mod(p + 1, 2). This is yet another example of the relevant role played by null runs. □

Extracting the coefficient of y m from (270) using the procedure that we have repeatedly used
throughout the paper yields

                                                 2 (1 − x2 ) x(1+Jp=0K) m
                   [y m ]S[p] (x, y) =                               m+1 .             (272)
                                           1 − Jp = 0Kx − 1 + Jp = 1K x2

This is the ogf enumerating the binary strings that contain exactly m nonnull p-parity runs
of ones and/or zeros.

Remark 30. In this remark we briefly discuss the consequences of our results in Section 5
for compositions. We start with the results in Section 5.1. Putting together Theorem 5.1
and the connection
                 P (224) between runs  of ones and zeros and compositions, we find that the
                                   n m

                                     1

                                     2
Thus, not only all results in Sections 2.1–2.7 —which, we remind, only concern runs of
ones— can be directly applied to the corresponding enumerations of n-strings with prescribed
quantities of nonnull runs of ones and/or zeros, but they can also be directly applied to the
corresponding enumerations of compositions of n with prescribed quantities of parts. We
give next some examples that recover known results due to other authors. We mainly focus
on generating functions, but of course recurrences and explicit results also follow from the
connections given.

Last but not least, Heubach and Mansour obtained many of their results for compositions as
a specialisation of a general theorem of theirs that gives the ogf of the number of compositions
with m parts in a general subset A ⊆ N [31, Thm. 2.1]. Through Theorems 5.1 and 5.2,
most ogfs for runs in the current paper can in principle be recovered by relying on Heubach
and Mansour’s theorem, and also new results for runs be obtained.
Some further comments about compositions and runs are given in Remark 36.                     □

                                                     76
6       Extensions to Probabilistic Runs of Ones and/or Ze-
        ros
Just as in Section 3, where we discussed the probabilistic extension of the enumerative results
in Section 2, we can extend the enumerative results in Section 5 to the same probabilistic
scenario: the case where the n-strings are outcomes from n iid Bernoulli random variables
with parameter q, where 0 < q < 1 is the probability of drawing a 1. As it may be intuited,
in this scenario there is no easy mapping between the results for runs of ones and the results
for runs of ones and zeros, i.e., there are no probabilistic analogues of Theorems 5.1 and 5.2,
and the expressions can get rather involved.
We mainly deal in this section with the extension of the results in Section 5.1 to the prob-
abilistic scenario. We also examine the extension of the special case in Section 5.3, as this
was previously analysed in detail by Wishart and Hirschfeld [69]. The same approach can
obviously be used to extend the results in Section 5.5, although we omit it here.
Like in Section 3, it is understood that all expressions in this section implicitly depend on q.


6.1     Probability that an n-String Contains Exactly m Nonnull (k ≤
        k)-Runs of Ones and/or Zeros
We call λk≤k (n, m) the probability that an n-string contains exactly m nonnull (k ≤ k)-runs
of ones and/or zeros. The obvious case is q = 1/2, in which λk≤k (n, m) = sk≤k (n, m)/2n .
This was in fact the approach followed in special cases of this expression by Bloom [8] and
by Suman [64], who produced λ1 (n, m) = s1 (n, m)/2n and λ≤k (n) = s≤k (n)/2n , respectively,
using explicit expressions —Suman’s results actually apply to the longest run of any b-ary
symbol in b-ary strings of length n. We examine next how to get λk≤k (n, m) for arbitrary q.


6.1.1     Recurrence

As per our notation conventions, we denote the joint probability that an n-string starts
                                                                               b
with b and contains exactly m nonnull (k ≤ k)-runs of ones and/or zeros by λk≤k   (n, m). By
the law of total probabilities, the probability that we wish to determine can be expressed in
terms of these joint probabilities as
                                          0

Remark 31. Schilling mentioned that a recurrence for λ≤k (n) = λ(k+1)≤n (n, 0) can be ob-
tained [54, p. 200] in a similar way as (165). While he did not furnish the recurrence, this
author indicated that the approximation λ≤k (n) ≈ π ≤k (n) —see Section 3.4— works well
for very large n when using q̌ = max(q, 1 − q) to compute π ≤k (n). Empirically, for fixed n,
the accuracy of this approximation increases as k increases.                                □

6.2     Probability that an n-String Contains Exactly m Nonnull Runs
        of Ones and/or Zeros
Let λ(n, m) be the probability that an n-string contains exactly m nonnull runs of ones
and/or zeros. This is a special case of the probability studied in the previous section with
k = 1 and k = n, and thus λ(n, m) = λ1≤n (n, m). Wishart and Hirschfeld previously studied
this problem in some detail [69].

7     Number of n-Strings of Hamming Weight r that Con-
      tain Prescribed Quantities of Nonnull Runs of Ones
      and/or Zeros Under Different Constraints
In this section we address the enumeration of the n-strings of Hamming weight r that contain
prescribed quantities of nonnull runs of ones and/or zeros under different constraints. This

                                             82
section is to Section 5 what Section 4 is to Section 2. One important implication of the
Hamming weight constraint is that, unlike in Section 5, no enumeration in this section is
related to a counterpart enumeration concerning the compositions of n. This is because an
n-string and its ones’ complement do not have the same Hamming weight in general. For the
same reason, we do not have analogues of Theorems 5.1 and 5.2 in this setting: the results
in this section are not related to the results in Section 4. Like in Sections 5 and 6, we only
consider nonnull runs of ones or zeros, and we approach the enumerations through mutual
recurrences. Importantly, the Hamming weight constraint creates a fundamental asymmetry
between these mutual recurrences like the one we observed in Section 6, which, in general,
makes it harder to obtain overall recurrences and generating functions. Due to this difficulty,
we only get explicit expressions in two particular cases.
Regarding previous work on this topic, Stevens [63] and Wald and Wolfowitz [66] first ad-
dressed the enumeration of the n-strings that have a prescribed number m of nonnull runs or
ones and/or zeros under the Hamming weight constraint. Their results were later rederived
by other authors [22, 56]. Also, Bateman [6], Schuster [56], Bloom [7], and Jackson (see [7])
tackled the enumeration of the n-strings of Hamming weight r whose longest nonnull run is
a (≤ k)-run of ones or zeros.


7.1     Number of n-Strings of Hamming Weight r that Contain Ex-
        actly m Nonnull (k ≤ k)-Runs of Ones and/or Zeros
In this section we enumerate the n-strings of Hamming weight r that contain exactly m
nonnull (k ≤ k)-runs of ones and/or zeros, which we denote by sk≤k (n, m, r). Thus, we
assume k ≥ 1.
As in Section 4.1, we first narrow down necessary condition (225) to take into account the
Hamming weight constraint.

Necessary Condition 7. (Existence of n-strings of Hamming weight r containing m nonnull
(k ≤ k)-runs of ones and/or zeros)
                                                                  !
                                                     n    r      n−r
            sk≤k (n, m, r) > 0 =⇒ 0 ≤ m ≤ min          ,     +            .       (308)
                                                     k    k         k

                                                                                             □

Importantly, unlike in Section 5.1.1 the mutual recurrences in (309) for the n-strings that
start with 0 or with 1 are not symmetric. This is analogous to what we saw in Section 6.
Because of this, we cannot simply input (309) into (313) and then directly work out a
recurrence for sk≤k (n, m, r). Therefore we do not attempt to obtain such a general recurrence,
but we see later that this asymmetry can be wrestled with in special cases.

7.2     Number of n-Strings of Hamming Weight r that Contain Ex-
        actly m Nonnull Runs of Ones and/or Zeros
We call s(n, m, r) the number of n-strings of Hamming weight r that contain exactly m
nonnull runs of ones and/or zeros (of arbitrary lengths, all strictly greater than zero).

                                                  85
This enumeration —in fact, the probability λ(n, m, r) = s(n, m, r)/ nr — was first given
                                                                             

by Stevens [63]. However, its relevance in the theory of runs is mainly due to Wald and
Wolfowitz, who independently derived the same result one year later [66] to use it as the ba-
sis of their runs-based hypothesis test for the identity between the distributions from which
two samples are drawn. The probability λ(n, m, r) was also rederived later in alternative
ways by several other authors, such as Gibbons [22] and Schuster [56]. The latter author,
who denotes the probability λ(n, m, r) by R, comments [56, Rem. 2]): “(. . . ) much of the
difficulty in studying the theoretical properties of R is due to the fact that R is not symmet-
rically defined (. . . )”. This difficulty, which was in fact the stumbling block preventing us
from producing a general recurrence in Section 7.1, also rears its ugly head in our approach
to this particular enumeration, although in a less severe manner.
As in previous cases, s(n, m, r) is a special case of our general analysis in Section 7.1 with
k = 1 and k = n, and therefore

                                    s(n, m, r) = s1≤n (n, m, r).                               (320)


7.2.1   Recurrence

In order to delve deeper into this special enumeration, we start by specialising recurrence (309)
to get

them, which allows us to produce a recurrence for s(n, m, r). To this end, we fist make (321)
valid for all values of the parameters by adding Jn = 0KJm = 0KJr = 0K to it. We then apply
this extended recurrence to itself, which yields

                                                   r
using (305) and q = r/n to see that, for large n, E(M     e n,r ) ≈ 1 + 2(n − r)r(n − 1)/n2 ≈
1 + 2(n − r)r/n, an expression that is well known [66, Eq. (12)].
This connection between λ(n, m) and λ(n, m, r) implies that Wishart and Hirschfeld’s work [69]
somehow predated that of Wald and Wolfowitz [66]: Wald and Wolfowitz’s test could, in
principle, have been based on the earlier results in [69] for large n.                     □




7.3     Number of n-Strings of Hamming Weight r Whose Longest
        Nonnull Run Is a (≤ k)-Run of Ones or Zeros
We denote by s≤k (n, r) the number of n-strings of Hamming weight r whose longest nonnull
run is a (≤ k)-run of ones or zeros, where k ≥ 1. Bateman [6] gave an explicit expression
for the complementary probability of λ≤k (n, r) = s≤k (n, r)/ nr , and tables for λk (n, r) =
λ≤k (n, r) − λ≤(k−1) (n, r) for small values of n, r and k. Schuster [56], Bloom [7], and Jackson
(see [7]) gave recurrences for s≤k (n, r). Schuster also extended and corrected Bateman’s
tables.
We can study this problem as a special case of the results in Section 7.1, because

                                 s≤k (n, r) = s(k+1)≤n (n, 0, r).                          (337)


7.3.1   Recurrence

Let us see how we can deduce a recurrence for s≤k (n, r) from the general expressions. Calling

Remark 33. At first sight, (341) may look unrelated to the recurrence for the same enu-
meration first cleverly surmised and then proved by Bloom [7, Eq. (9)] —in his notation,
s≤k (n, r) = Ck+1 (n − r, r). However, if we obtain s≤k (n, r) − s≤k (n − 1, r) using (341), then
we essentially recover Bloom’s recurrence:
                              k
                              X                                k

In Bloom’s recurrence, index i only affects the second argument of Ct (·, ·) whereas above
it affects both arguments of s≤k (·, ·). This just a notational artifact: in Bloom’s notation
the addition of the two arguments yields the length of the binary string, and thus the
homogeneous part of both recurrences is exactly the same. The inhomogeneous term in
Bloom’s recurrence does not always equal the inhomogeneous term in (342), which simply
indicates a different initialisation strategy: both recurrences return the same values.
Finally, if we obtain s≤k (n, r) − s≤k (n − 1, r − 1) using (342), we also essentially recover
Jackson’s recurrence [7, Eq. (13)]:
                                                                                  
      s≤k (n, r) = s≤k n − 1, r − 1 + s≤k n − 1, r − s≥k n − (k + 2), r − (k + 1)

                       i=1
                                                                     
                   + 2 Jn = 0K − Jn = 1K Jr = 0K − 2 Jn = 1K − Jn = 2K Jr = 1K.           (343)

Again the homogeneous parts of (343) and [7, Eq. (13)] are identical, but the inhomogeneous
parts are different. At any rate, both recurrences deliver the same numerical values. The
number of recurrent calls made by (341), (342) and (343) is k 2 , 2k + 1 and 5, respectively.
Thus, (343) is the most efficient recurrence when k > 2, only improved by (341) when k = 1
or k = 2.
To conclude, we should mention that Schuster was the first author who found a recurrence
for s≤k (n, r) —in fact, for λ≤k (n, r) = s≤k (n, r)/ nr [56, Cor. 5.4]. His recurrence depends
on an involved ancillary function, and it does not seem to bear a close relationship to the
recurrences discussed above.                                                                  □




7.3.2   Generating Functions

We now obtain S ≤k (x, z) = n,r s≤k (n, r) xn z r . We can do so using (341), (342) or (343)
                             P
without further ado, since none of these equivalent recurrences contains n-dependent sum-
mations and all of them are valid for all arguments. Let us use (343), as it involves the least
amount of algebra. Multiplying this recurrence on both sides by xn z r and then adding over
n and r we can see that
                                 2 − x(1 + z) + xk+2 (z + z k+1 ) − xk+1 (1 + z k+1 )
                 S ≤k (x, z) =                                                        .   (344)
                                   1 − x(1 + z) + xk+2 (z + z k+1 ) − x2(k+1) z k+1

The reader may verify that the same ogf is obtained from recurrences (341) or (342). Using
instead Jackson’s original recurrence [7, Eq. (13)] we get a somewhat simpler ogf:

                                        1 − xk+1 (1 + z k+1 ) + x2(k+1) z k+1
                   S ≤k (x, z) =                                                    .     (345)
                                   1 − x(1 + z) + xk+2 (z + z k+1 ) − x2(k+1) z k+1

Using any of these two ogfs, we may obtain s≤k (n, r) = [xn z r ]S ≤k (x, z) using a computer
algebra system.

In order to extract the coefficient of xn z r from S ≤k (x, z) we define

which we use to rewrite (344) as

       S ≤k (x, z) = a(0,0,0) (x, z) + a(1,0,0) (x, z) − a(1,k+1,0) (x, z) − a(1,k+1,k+1) (x, z).   (347)

Applying the negative binomial theorem (twice) and then the binomial theorem (three times),
we can expand (346) as
                                                 

Thus, the coefficient of x z in this expression is obtained by determining the nonnegative

This implies that t ≤ min(⌊(n − v)/(2(k + 1))⌋, ⌊(r − w)/(k + 1)⌋), l ≤ ⌊(r − w − (k + 1)t)/k⌋
and i ≤ ⌊(n − 2(k + 1)t − v)/(k + 1)⌋, whereas, for any (t, l, i) triple, j is determined by the

                                                          p       i      j       l

where the upper limits of the summations on t, l, and i are given by the three aforementioned
inequalities, and p and j are determined using the two equations above. We do not need to
check for nonnegativity of p and j, as if that were the case the corresponding terms in (349)
would cancel out. Using (349) and (347), we have an explicit triple-summation expression
for s≤k (n, r) = [xn z r ]S ≤k (x, y), comparable to the triple-summation expression originally
given by Bateman for λ≥k (n, r) [6, p. 100].

Remark 34. The ogfs (344) or (345), or the explicit expression in the previous section, allow
us to address the same problem that motivated Bloom’s work in [7]. Bloom’s research was
spurred by his desire to determine the accuracy of the following assertion by M. Gardner
in [21, p. 124]: “(. . . ) a shuffled deck of cards will contain coincidences. For instance, almost
always there will be a clump of six or seven cards of the same color.” Gardner, like Bloom,
uses the word “clump” to mean a run. More specifically, what Bloom calls a “k-clump”
is a run of length k or longer of either colour of the deck, and he wishes to determine the
likelihood that a shuffled deck will contain a 6-clump.
If we just look at the colour of the cards, then there is a bijection between the shuffles
of a standard deck and the binary strings of length 52 and Hamming weight 26 (where 0
means “red” and 1 means “black”, or vice versa). The number of n-strings devoid of (≥
6)-runs of ones or zeros is s≤5 (52, 26) = 265, 692, 662, 174, 100, and, thus, the probability
that a shuffled deck of cards drawn uniformly at random contains at least one 6-clump is
1 − s≤5 (52, 26)/ 52
                  26
                     = 0.464241.
This certainly coincides with Bloom’s analysis, and it confirms this author’s finding that
Gardner’s claim is hardly supported by the numbers. Bloom also used the expectation (384)

                                                     92
in Section 8.2 as an indirect way to support his findings. However, as seems to have happened
so many times in the history of the theory of runs, this author missed Bateman’s results.
Had he not missed them, he would simply have implemented the explicit expression given
by Bateman [6, p. 100] to answer his question. But had he done so, we would have missed
on his insightful paper.
Lastly, the tables for λ≤k (n, r) = s≤k (n, r)/ nr given by Schuster [56, pp. 112–116] —in his
                                                  

notation, λ≤k (n, r) = FM (k, n − r, r)— can also be easily reproduced through Bateman’s
explicit expression (or, of course, through our own explicit expression or through the ogfs
above).                                                                                      □

of (308), is given next.

8       Number of Runs Under Different Constraints Over
        All n-Strings and Over Restricted Subsets
Our main goal in this section is enumerating how many runs under different constraints are
found over all n-strings. The most basic results are the enumerations of the (k ≤ k)-runs over
all n-strings in Section 8.1 and over all n-strings with Hamming weight r in Section 8.2, which
we then specialise to several particular cases. In Section 8.3 we study the number of runs
over the n-strings enumerated in Sections 2.1 and 2.8. These same problems are revisited in
Section 8.4, but with the goal of determining the number of runs of ones and zeros instead.
Several of the questions addressed in this section, or variations of them, were previously
studied by Marbe [42], Gold [24], Cochran [13], Bloom [7], Sinha and Sinha [60, 61], Makri

                                                       95
et al. [40, 41], Nyblom [49], and Grimaldi and Heubach [28] —and perhaps by other authors
we do not know of, since these are fairly common problems. In fact, many of the authors
just mentioned were not aware of previous results.
Since runs of zeros appear at some points throughout this section, the reader is again re-
minded that, unless explicitly said otherwise, the term “run” without any qualifier refers to
a run of ones.


8.1    Number of (k ≤ k)-Runs Over All n-Strings
We denote the number of (k ≤ k)-runs over all n-strings by ρk≤k (n). Considering (1), it is
possible to compute ρk≤k (n) by relying on the enumeration wk≤k (n, m) studied in Section 2.1
as follows:                                
                                            n+1
                                            k+1
                                           X
                               ρk≤k (n) =       m wk≤k (n, m).                          (362)
                                             m=1

This summation requires an explicit expression for wk≤k (n, m), which we have not provided.
However, it is also possible to obtain ρk≤k (n) directly from the ogf (16):

                                                   ∂Wk≤k (x, y)
                               ρk≤k (n) = [xn ]                           .               (363)
                                                        ∂y
                                                                    y=1

See that this is essentially the same as the computation of the first moment with a pgf —cf.
Section 3.2.4. From (16) we have that
                                                                  
                             ∂Wk≤k (x, y)      (1 − x) xk − xk+1
                                             =                      .                  (364)
                                  ∂y                (1 − 2x)2
                                          y=1

By applying the negative binomial theorem, we can expand (364) as follows:

               ∂Wk≤k (x, y)
                                                                                 
                                      k      k+1        k+1        k+2
                                                                         X t + 1 t t
                                    = x −x         −x         +x                    2x.   (365)
                   ∂y                                                     t≥0
                                                                                t
                              y=1

To extract the coefficient of xn we have to solve four equations for the nonnegative index t:
a) n = k + t, b) n = k + 1 + t, c) n = k + 1 + t, and d) n = k + 2 + t. When each of these
equations has a solution, from (365) their respective contributions to the coefficient of xn
are:

  a) (n − k + 1) 2n−k

  b) −(n − k) 2n−(k+1)

                                                   96
  c) −(n − k) 2n−(k+1)

  d) (n − k − 1) 2n−(k+2)

   • If k + 2 ≤ n, as k ≤ k there is a solution in each of the four cases, so all four
     contributions must be added.

   • If k + 1 = n only the first three equations have a solution, but all four contributions
     can still be added because the fourth one is zero anyway.

   • If k ≥ n then the last two equations do not have a solution, and we have the following
     cases for the first two:

         ◦ If k > n no equation has a solution.
         ◦ If k < n the first two equations have a solution, so the first two contributions
           must be added.
         ◦ If k = n only the first equation has a solution, but the first two contributions can
           still be added because the second one is zero anyway.

Collecting all these contributions we finally can see that

            ρk≤k (n) = (n − k + 2) 2n−(k+1) Jk ≤ nK − (n − k + 1) 2n−(k+2) Jk < nK.          (366)

Observe that (368) is valid for all 0 ≤ k ≤ k and n ≥ 1 —for example, it can enumerate the
null runs over all n-strings if desired.
Although we will not show it here, (366) can also be obtained by establishing a recurrence for
ρk≤k (n) from first principles (i.e, without resorting to any of the results in previous sections),
and then solving it directly by unrolling it. In any case, the approach that we have given
above is quicker and cleaner.


8.1.1   Special Cases

In this section we discuss several specialisations of ρk≤k (n). We denote the number of k-runs
over all n-strings by ρk (n), and the number of nonnull runs (of arbitrary lengths, all strictly
greater than zero) over all n-strings by ρ(n). These enumerations are special cases of our
analysis in the previous section, as we can write ρk (n) = ρk≤k (n) and ρ(n) = ρ1≤n (n). Thus,
using (366) we have that

                        ρk (n) = (n − k + 3) 2n−(k+2) Jk < nK + Jk = nK,                     (367)

and
                                      ρ(n) = (n + 1) 2n−2 .                                  (368)

                                                97
Next, we denote the number of (≥ k)-runs and of nonnull (≤ k)-runs over all n-strings by
ρ≥k (n) and by ρ≤k (n), respectively. Once again, these are special cases of our analysis in the
previous section, because we can write ρ≥k (n) = ρk≤n (n) and ρ≤k (n) = ρ1≤k (n). Therefore,
from (366) we have
                            ρ≥k (n) = (n − k + 2) 2n−(k+1) Jk ≤ nK                         (369)
and
                        ρ≤k (n) = ρ(n) − (n − k + 1) 2n−(k+2) Jk < nK,                    (370)
where ρ(n) is given by (368).

Remark 35. Leaving aside Marbe’s formula for a moment —see below— the first author
who produced (367) was Gold [24, 14], in his analysis of the predictability of two-state
meteorological series. This author gave ρk (n)/2n as the expected number of k-runs in an n-
string drawn uniformly at random —he actually obtained twice the value of this expectation,
as
Pnhe addressed runs     of ones and zeros jointly. Gold arrived at his result by combining
                     n−1
   k=1 k ρk (n) = n2     (see Remark 41) and ρk−1 (n − 1) = ρk (n), an identity which can
easily be argued. Gold’s expectation was then extended by Cochran [13, Eq. (5)] to the case
where the bits are not equally likely —Cochran, who was studying the spread of diseases
in plants arranged in rows, acknowledges Marbe’s priority regarding his result [42], but he
indicates that the original proof [43, p. 9] omits essential steps, and gives his own proof by
induction. Cochran’s expectation in [13, Eq. (5)] is in fact (137) in Section 3.2.4, which
simplifies to (367) using q = 1/2 and multiplying by 2n .
We briefly discuss other approaches for the derivation of some of these enumerations, as
we retrace the work of other authors who previously dealt with them. These authors were
unaware of the aforementioned results. As indicated by Sinha and Sinha [60] and by Makri
and Psillakis [40, Eq. (9)], one way to obtain ρk (n) is

                                              k+1 ⌋
                                             ⌊X
                                              n+1


                                  ρk (n) =            m wk (n, m),                        (371)
                                             m=1

which is the specialisation of (362). However, this is not an easy approach —consider
using (31) in (371). Tellingly, Makri et al. were only able to evaluate an expression similar
to (371) to obtain (367) [41, Eq. (7), a = e] by using an earlier probabilistic result of
theirs, but not by using their own explicit expression for wk (n, m) [40, Eq. (8)] in (371).
As mentioned at the end of the previous section, it is possible to establish a recurrence for
ρk≤k (n) ab initio. In the special case k = k = k, this recurrence is

                                ρk (n) = 2 ρk (n − 1) + 2n−(k+2)                          (372)

for k + 2 ≤ n, whereas ρn−1 (n) = 2 and ρn (n) = 1. Recurrence (372) and explicit ex-
pression (367) were worked out from first principles by Sinha and Sinha in [61] —according
to [60], an earlier appearance of these authors’ results regarding ρk (n) is in K. Sinha’s PhD

                                                98
thesis [59]. Sinha and Sinha cite [14] in passing in [61], without realising that Cochran’s
article already contains the closed-form solution (367) they find. Makri et al. [41, Eq. (9),
a = e] also noticed recurrence (372) after finding the explicit expression (367) for ρk (n). We
would also like to mention that we gave two other ab initio approaches to the problem of
finding ρk (n) in [4].
We should mention that, after determining the closed-form expression (367) for ρk (n) through
any of the methods just mentioned, one can then obtain ρk≤k (n) = kk=k ρk (n) —which only
                                                                   P

involves summations of the form k ak and k k ak . While this makes the approach in the
                                 P           P
previous section unnecessary, we believe that the method that we have adopted is interesting
because it connects with the enumerations in Section 2. It also allows for a consistent
methodology throughout the paper —cf. Sections 2.1, 3.1, 4.1, 5.1, 6.1, 7.1 and 9.1.
As regards ρ(n), this enumeration may be obtained P in a number of different ways as well.
One possibility is to use ρk (n) to compute ρ(n) = nk=1 ρk (n), as pointed out by Sinha and
Sinha [61]. Using (367) we can thus write
                                          n−1

Finally, we make a few comments about ρ≥k (n). Expression (369) can also be obtained from
expectation (156) in Section 3.3.4, by setting q = 1/2 and then multiplying by 2n . Bloom
gave (369) divided by 2n−1 —i.e., the expected number of (≥ k)-runs of ones and/or zeros in
an n-string drawn uniformly at random [7, Eq. (12)]. He most likely also used a probabilistic

                                                 99
approach, but he gives no details. Another way to obtain ρ≥k (n) is by specialising (362) with
k = k and k = n, which yields ρ≥k (n) = ⌊(n+1)(k+1)⌋
                                         P
                                            m=1       m w≥k (n, m). Although it is again very
difficult to handle this summation using an explicit expression for w≥k (n, m), such as (45),
a probabilistic version of this expression was used by Makri et al. to obtain (369) [41, Eq.
(7), a = g]. These authors also deduced a recurrence working backwards from the explicit
expression [41, Eq. (9), a = g].                                                             □



Remark 36. There are some immediate connections between the results in this section and
compositions. For example, the total number of parts in all compositions of n is the same as
the total number of nonnull runs over all n-strings, ρ(n), and thus given by (368). In similar
fashion, the total number of parts equal to k ≥ 1 in all compositions of n equals the total
number of runs of length k over all n-strings, ρk (n), which is given by (367). In the special
case k = 1, both recurrence (372) and closed-form expression (367) were given by Chin
et al. [11, Eqs. (1) and (2)]. These authors studied a problem concerning Cuisenaire rods
(number rods) which is equivalent to enumerating all parts equal to 1 over all compositions
of n.                                                                                        □




8.1.2     OEIS

We report next sequences connected to the enumerations in this section that are found in
the OEIS:

   • All sequences emanating from ρk (n), ρ≥k (n), and ρ(n) are in the OEIS:
        ρk (n + k) is A045623 (number of 1’s in all compositions of n + 1).
        ρ≥k (n + k) and ρ(n + 1) are A001792.

   • Sequences emanating from ρ≤k (n):
        ρ≤1 (n + 1) is A045623 —just like ρk (n + k).
        ρ≤2 (n + 1) is A106472.
        ρ≤3 (n) is A386878.


8.2      Number of (k ≤ k)-Runs Over All n-Strings of Hamming Weight r
In this section we obtain the total number of (k ≤ k)-runs over all n-strings of Hamming
weight r, which we denote by ρk≤k (n, r). We can obtain this quantity directly from (192) as
follows:
                                                  ∂Wk≤k (x, y, z)
                          ρk≤k (n, r) = [xn z r ]                 .                    (375)
                                                      ∂y
                                                               y=1




                                                100
From (192) we have that
                                                                             
                       ∂Wk≤k (x, y, z)              (1 − xz) (xz)k − (xz)k+1
                                                  =                            .           (376)
                                ∂y                        (1 − x − xz)2
                                            y=1

                                                                             
By rewriting this expression as ((xz)k − (xz)k+1 )/ (1 − xz)(1 − x/(1 − xz))2 and then
applying the negative binomial theorem twice, we can expand it as follows:

        ∂Wk≤k (x, y, z)
                                                                        
                                                         XX t + 1 s + t t

              ∂y                                          t≥0 s≥0

                          y=1

To extract the coefficient of xn z r we just have to find the nonnegative indices t and s that
fulfil two sets of equations: on the one hand, n = k + t + s and r = k + s; on the other hand,
n = k + 1 + t + s and r = k + 1 + s. The solutions of the first set are t = n − r and s = r − k,
whereas the solutions of the second one are t = n − r and s = r − k − 1. For a solution to
exist, both sets require n ≥ r, whereas r ≥ k and r ≥ k + 1 are required in the first and
second set, respectively. From these considerations and (377) we thus have that
                                                                
                                              n−k         n−k−1
                  ρk≤k (n, r) = (n − r + 1)          −                  Jn ≥ rK.          (378)
                                               r−k        r−k−1

Observe that the constraints on r are taken care of by the binomial coefficients. Like (366),
this expression is valid for all 0 ≤ k ≤ k and n ≥ 1, apart from all r.


8.2.1     Special Cases

We consider next several specialisations of ρk≤k (n, r). We denote the number of (≥ k)-runs
and the total number of nonnull runs over all n-strings of Hamming weight r by ρ≥k (n, r) and

and ρ(n, r) = ρ1≤n (n, r) = ρ≥1 (n, r). Thus, from (378) we see that

8.3    Number of Nonnull Runs Over All n-Strings that Contain
       Exactly m Nonnull Runs Under Different Constraints
Next, we deal with the problems of enumerating the number of nonnull runs over all n-
strings that contain exactly m nonnull (k ≤ k)-runs, or exactly m nonnull p-parity runs.
In keeping with our notation conventions, we call these quantities ρk≤k (n, m) and ρ[p] (n, m),
respectively.
These problems are not as elementary as the ones we have dealt with in the previous two
sections. Relatively simple explicit expressions do not seem possible in general, although they
are achievable in particular cases —see Section 8.3.3. However, we can obtain recurrence
relations and generating functions for these enumerations by relying on their counterparts
in Sections 2.1 and 2.8, i.e., wk≤k (n, m) and w[p] (n, m), respectively.

                                               102
8.3.1   Number of Nonnull Runs Over All n-Strings that Contain Exactly m
        Nonnull (k ≤ k)-Runs

We obtain first a recurrence for ρk≤k (n, m), by splitting the contributions due n-strings with
different initial bit. As indicated, we assume k ≥ 1. The n-strings that start with 0 contribute
ρk≤k (n − 1, m), to ρk≤k (n, m). On the other hand, the n-strings that start with a nonnull
i-run with k ≤ i ≤ k contribute ρk≤k (n − (i + 1), m − 1) runs plus the wk≤k (n − (i + 1), m − 1)
initial runs themselves. If they start with a nonnull i-run with i < k or i > k, then they
contribute ρk≤k (n − (i + 1), m) runs plus the wk≤k (n − (i + 1), m) initial runs. This yields
the recurrence
                                    k 
                                    X                                                            
 ρk≤k (n, m) = ρk≤k (n − 1, m) +            ρk≤k (n − (i + 1), m − 1) + wk≤k (n − (i + 1), m − 1)
                                    i=k
                                    k−1                                                    
                                    X
                                +           ρk≤k (n − (i + 1), m) + wk≤k (n − (i + 1), m)
                                    i=1

                                    i=k+1

                       ρk≤k (1, m) = Jm = 0KJk > 1K + Jm = 1KJk = 1K                            (386)

   ρk≤k (1, m) = ρk≤k 0, m + ρk≤k − 1, m − Jk = 1K + wk≤k − 1, m − Jk = 1K .          (387)

We wish (387) to equal (386). Taking (11) and (12) into account, equality between these
two expressions is fulfilled for all m and 1 ≤ k ≤ k by choosing

                                ρk≤k (−1, m) = ρk≤k (0, m) = 0,                                 (388)

which are therefore the initial values of (385).
Recurrence (385) also allows us to find the ogf Rk≤k (x, y) = n,m ρk≤k (n, m) xn y m in terms of

8.3.2   Number of Nonnull Runs Over All n-Strings that Contain Exactly m
        Nonnull p-Parity Runs

We now give a recurrence relation for ρ[p] (n, m). Following the same strategy as in the
previous section, the n-strings that start with 0 contribute ρ[p] (n − 1, m) runs to ρ[p] (n, m).
As for the n-strings that start with 1, if they start with an odd i-run then they contribute
ρ[p] (n−(i+1), m−Jp = 1K) runs plus the w[p] (n−(i+1), m−Jp = 1K) initial runs themselves.
If they start with an even i-run then they contribute ρ[p] (n − (i + 1), m − Jp = 0K) runs plus
the w[p] (n − (i + 1), m − Jp = 0K) initial runs. This yields the recurrence
                                              n
                                              X

                              i=1

To initialise (390) we use the value at n = 1, which, by inspection, is

                        ρ[p] (1, m) = Jp = 0KJm = 0K + Jp = 1KJm = 1K.                        (391)

           ρ[p] (1, m) = ρ[p] (0, m) + ρ[p] (−1, m − Jp = 1K) + w[p] (−1, m − Jp = 1K).       (392)

We want (392) to equal (391). Taking (1) and (103) into account, we see that equality
between the two expressions is achieved for all m by choosing

                                     ρ[p] (−1, m) = ρ[p] (0, m) = 0,

which are therefore the initial values of (390).

                                                  105
8.4     Number of Nonnull Runs of Ones and/or Zeros Over All n-
        Strings that Contain Exactly m Nonnull Runs of Ones Under
        Different Constraints
In this section we address the problems of enumerating the number of nonnull runs of ones
and zeros over all n-strings that contain exactly m nonnull (k ≤ k)-runs of ones or exactly
m nonnull p-parity runs of ones. We call these two quantities βk≤k (n, m) and β[p] (n, m),
respectively. Special cases of both problems were previously studied by Nyblom [49] and by
Grimaldi and Heubach [28].
These two enumerations can be obtained in a similar way as the equivalent enumerations in
the previous section. The main difference is that, rather than being respectively based on
recurrences for wk≤k (n, m) and w[p] (n, m) like in Section 8.3, the two recurrences are now
based on mutual recurrences for the same two quantities constrained to the initial bit of the
n-strings —just like the ones used in Section 5, but for runs of ones only. The asymmetry
of such mutual recurrences implies that deriving ogfs, although certainly feasible, is rather
laborious —cf. (319). Therefore we content ourselves with only providing recurrences for
βk≤k (n, m) and β[p] (n, m).


8.4.1   Number of Nonnull Runs of Ones and/or Zeros Over All n-Strings that
        Contain Exactly m Nonnull (k ≤ k)-Runs of Ones

As indicated we assume k ≥ 1. We begin by finding mutual recurrences for the number
of n-strings that start with b ∈ {0, 1} and contain exactly m (k ≤ k)-runs of ones, which
                 b
we denote by wk≤k   (n, m). If an n-string starts with an i-run of zeros then it contributes
  1                 0

                                  0                  1

   0
wk≤k  (n − i, m). Therefore

to obtain a recurrence for wk≤k (n, m) through this approach, due to the asymmetry between
the two mutual recurrences in (396) —similarly to the enumeration in Section 5.1. Moreover,
this alternative approach also makes the derivation of the ogf (16) less simple.
After these preliminaries we are ready to tackle the enumeration of βk≤k (n, m). We begin by

                                                                           0

9     Number of Ones in Runs Under Different Constraints
      Over All n-Strings and Over Restricted Subsets
Our main goal in this section is enumerating how many ones are found in runs under different
constraints over all n-strings. The most basic result, in Section 9.1, is the enumeration of the
ones in (k ≤ k)-runs over all n-strings, of which we consider several relevant special cases.
We also study in Section 9.2 the number of ones in the n-strings enumerated in Sections 2.1
and 2.8. Several of the enumerations considered here were previously studied by Makri et
al. [41], Nyblom [49], and Grimaldi and Heubach [28].


9.1    Number of Ones in (k ≤ k)-Runs Over All n-Strings
We denote by tk≤k (n) the number of ones in (k ≤ k)-runs over all n-strings. First of all, it is
not possible to establish a simple formula for tk≤k (n) by directly exploiting the main results
in Section 2.1 —i.e., using formulas parallel to (362) or to (363). It is possible, though, to
obtain tk≤k (n) in a more roundabout way by using the enumeration in Section 2.2 as follows:

9.1.1      Special Cases

We denote the number of ones in (≥ k)-runs, the number of ones in (≤ k)-runs, and the
total number of ones over all n-strings by t≥k (n), t≤k (n) and t(n), respectively. These are
special cases of the result in the previous section, since we can write t≥k (n) = tk≤n (n),
t≤k (n) = t1≤k (n), and t(n) = t1≤n (n). Thus, using (412) we have that
                       t≥k (n) = n(k + 1) − k(k − 1) 2n−(k+1) Jk ≤ nK,

9.2      Number of Ones Over All n-Strings that Contain Exactly m
         Nonnull Runs Under Different Constraints
This section is the equivalent of Section 8.3. Here we deal with the problems of enumerating
how many ones are found over all n-strings that contain exactly m nonnull (k ≤ k)-runs or
exactly m nonnull p-parity runs. In keeping with our notation conventions, we call these
quantities tk≤k (n, m) and t[p] (n, m), respectively. Special cases of these problems were pre-
viously studied by Nyblom [49] and by Grimaldi and Heubach [28].
Again, while relatively simple explicit expressions do not seem possible in general, we give
recurrence relations and generating functions for these enumerations by relying on their
counterparts in Sections 2.1 and 2.8, i.e., wk≤k (n, m) and w[p] (n, m), respectively. We must
mention though that explicit expressions are possible in some particular cases —see Re-
mark 43 and Section 9.2.3.

                                                 111
9.2.1   Number of Ones Over All n-Strings That Contain Exactly m Nonnull
        (k ≤ k)-Runs

We obtain first a recurrence for tk≤k (n, m), the number of ones over all n-strings that contain
exactly m nonnull (k ≤ k)-runs. As indicated, we assume k ≥ 1. Following the customary
strategy, the n-strings that start with 0 contribute tk≤k (n − 1, m) runs to tk≤k (n, m). As for
the n-strings that start with 1, if they start with a nonnull i-run with k ≤ i ≤ k then they
contribute tk≤k (n−(i+1), m−1) ones plus the i wk≤k (n−(i+1), m−1) initial ones themselves.
If they start with a nonnull i-run with i < k or i > k then they contribute tk≤k (n−(i+1), m)
ones plus the i wk≤k (n − (i + 1), m) initial ones. This yields the recurrence

Remark 42. Nyblom [49, Thm. 3.1] gave a recurrence to compute the number of zeros in n-
strings devoid of (≥ k)-runs, which he denotes by ρk (n). We can also obtain this enumeration
as a special case of our results in this section by using t≥k (n, 0) = tk≤n (n, 0), which allows us
to write ρk (n) = n w≥k (n, 0) − t≥k (n, 0). Also, this expression is valid for all k ≥ 1 whereas
the recurrence in [49] requires k ≥ 3.                                                            □




9.2.2   Number of Ones Over All n-Strings That Contain Exactly m Nonnull
        p-Parity Runs

Finally, we find a recurrence relation for t[p] (n, m). The n-strings that start with 0 contribute
t[p] (n−1, m) ones to t[p] (n, m). As for the n-strings that start with 1, if they start with an odd
i-run then they contribute t[p] (n−(i+1), m−Jp = 1K) ones plus the i w[p] (n−(i+1), m−Jp =
1K) initial ones themselves. If they start with an even nonnull i-run then they contribute
t[p] (n − (i + 1), m − Jp = 0K) runs plus the i w[p] (n − (i + 1), m − Jp = 0K) initial runs. This
yields the recurrence

                                                    114
10     Conclusions
We have studied fundamental enumeration problems concerning runs in binary strings, where
the runs conform to Mood’s criterion [44]. While many of these problems had been solved
over the years by different authors through a variety of methods, we felt that there were
gaps in the literature, and that a uniform, systematic treatment would be beneficial to bring
together and connect the many contributions to the topic. We believe that the notation con-
ventions that we have chosen, even if necessarily unorthodox when it comes to probabilities
(in order to accommodate recurrences and pgfs), are effective when it comes to displaying
the relationships between enumerations concerning runs of ones, runs of ones and/or zeros,
and compositions, their probabilistic extensions, and their associated generating functions.
We consider that a relevant contribution of this paper is the identification of the existence
of null runs of ones (0-runs). Probably due to their lack of practical use, null runs seem
to have escaped the attention of previous authors. Nevertheless, we have seen that runs
of ones of zero length are essential to connect enumerations of runs of ones and zeros and
enumerations of runs of ones (Theorems 5.1 and 5.2). Null runs also naturally emerge in
problems involving the longest run of ones (Sections 2.6 and 4.3).
Hopefully, we have been able to show that approaching runs-related problems by means of
recurrences and generating functions is, for the most part, more straightforward than doing
so by means of direct combinatorial analysis methods. The exception to this rule may be
some enumeration problems with a Hamming weight constraint. We are not reinventing the
wheel here, since, as discussed in the introduction, this is an old and venerable approach
—even when it comes to studying runs. However, this powerful technique has probably not
been exploited to its full advantage in this area of research.
It would be naive to think that we have exhausted all interesting runs-related problems that
one can address through the strategy adopted in this paper. Extensions to probabilistic sce-
narios —briefly discussed in Sections 3 and 6 and at some other points— can be investigated
much further, following in the steps of previous authors who went beyond the iid case to
study Markov dependencies [6, 1, 5, 20]. Many purely enumerative problems not addressed
here also merit consideration, such as for example joint enumerations —see [26, Sec. 2.4.9].
Of course, explicit expressions based on binomial coefficients have numerical limitations for
large n. However, the generating functions that we have provided can definitely be helpful
in further studies about the asymptotics the enumerations studied here. Austin and Guy [3],
Sedgewick and Flajolet [19], Bloom [8], Suman [64], Prodinger [52], and other authors, pre-
viously followed this approach in some special scenarios. Another interesting route in terms
of asymptotics could be using moment generating functions instead of probability generating
functions, as done by Wishart and Hirschfeld [69] in one particular problem. We should also
remark that the mutual recurrences strategy on which the results in Sections 5, 6, and 7
hinge —which was first used by Wishart and Hirschfeld in a probabilistic context [69]— can
be readily extended to the enumeration of runs of different kinds in b-ary strings with b ≥ 2.
Some of these b-ary generalisations were already given by previous authors [15, 64], although

                                             115
not through the procedure we just mentioned. Any of the research avenues sketched above
may prove fruitful: in Schilling’s words, “the variety of potential applications of runs theory
is virtually boundless” [54].
The problems addressed in this paper contain a very rich structure in terms of the many
distinct number sequences that they encompass —many of them listed in Sloane’s On-Line
Encyclopedia of Integer Sequences, but many others yet unexplored. The omissions may be
as relevant as the inclusions: observe that few sequences corresponding to enumerations with
m ≥ 2 are in the OEIS. The many connections unearthed thanks to the inestimable help of
the OEIS may open up new uses for the diverse formulas and results given here.
To conclude, the recurrences
                        √      for runs of ones that we have produced compel us to say that,
in the same sense that −1 exists as a mathematical object, a binary string with length −1
does also have some kind of fleeting existence. Intriguingly, we are justified in stating that
the number of binary strings of length −1 that are devoid of runs of ones of length k ≥ 0 is
exactly one —in the same sense that we accept that there is a single binary string of length 0
(empty binary string). We have also seen that, when binary strings are drawn uniformly at
random with probability 0 < q < 1 of drawing a 1, the probability of a (or rather, “the”)
binary string of length −1 without runs of ones of length k ≥ 0 must be 1/(1 − q) > 1.
The majority of recurrences for runs of ones that we have given would not be correct for all
n ≥ 1 and for all valid values of the parameters without similar puzzling assertions being
true. Had we ignored or tried to skirt around these strange-looking facts, then most of those
recurrences would have been less general and more piecemeal —i.e., only valid for certain
ranges of the parameters and/or requiring ad-hoc initialisation. This fragmentary character
is in fact present in many recurrences concerning runs (or compositions) previously given by
other authors [2, 12, 54, 8, 49, 48]. We are not the first ones to observe this phenomenon:
the necessity of considering a binary string of length −1 in recurrences involving runs of ones
was implicit already in the work of of Austin and Guy [3].
